# VOLUME 05 OPEN THREADS — after Continuation 0013, Chapter 324, Saturday 27 May 2017

**Read with the "VOLUME 05, CONTINUATION 0013 — CHAPTERS 315–324" block at the end of `state/continuity.md`, which carries the canon for these ten chapters and governs wherever the two differ, and with the "VOLUME 05, CONTINUATION 0012 — CHAPTERS 305–314" block above it, which still governs everything those ten chapters did. **VOLUME 05 IS NOT CLOSED AND IS NOT DECLARED CLOSED. THE SERIES IS AT 324 OF 720. NOTHING IN CONTINUATION 0013 RESOLVED ANYTHING AND NOTHING IN IT MAY BE UNDONE, IMPROVED ON, RESTATED OR ANSWERED. EVERY DAY-COUNT BELOW IS CALCULATED FROM ITS ANCHOR ON THE DAY IN QUESTION AND NOT CARRIED FORWARD FROM A SUMMARY: at Saturday 27 May 2017 the blank line under the nineteenth is nine hundred and thirteen days, the card in the slot is eight hundred and twenty-seven, and the letter in the tray is eight hundred and twenty-nine.**

## 1. A morning lost over a downpipe

- **MAJOR TURN: A WORKING MORNING IS BROKEN OFF BY SOMETHING SMALL AND ORDINARY, A MAN PUTS A PROP UNDER THE RESULT WITHOUT BEING ASKED, THE MORNING GOES ON, AND THE ONLY PERSON WHO HEARS ANY OF IT IS SOMEONE WHO WAS NOT THERE.** A man in a fleece, who goes to the works, reverses his van a foot to get light on a wall at the low end of Peverell Street and takes a downpipe, two brackets and about two foot of pipe off a rendered return. The top plate behind the bracket has blown out into a crumbly ring the size of a closed fist, which means the water has been behind that paint for years.
- He cannot do that house and does not try: what is in his bag is a fascia bracket, and that house has no fascia on it, the wall is engineering brick at the bottom, and the last job of the round wants a tower and is booked for the week after. He lays the pipe along the channel so nothing hangs over a footway, does the other eight, and is back on that pavement at half past twelve.
- The man in his thirties, passing with his bag, spends about five minutes on it unasked: a foot of scaffold out of the back of his van, split, a two-by-four piece stood on end where the render returns, the pipe leaned onto it, and about two foot of wire off a coil round the pipe and the bracket and the render. **He said before and after that it is a prop, not a repair, and that the wall wants opening up in the autumn.**
- **Neither man said anything about it afterwards, and the chapter gives the real reason: neither would have expected it of the other, and having watched it happen neither had anything to put on it.** The man in a fleece finished at about ten past six, and nobody telephoned him about any of it: he is on nobody's list in this borough and nobody in that street has any idea he was on it that morning.
- The woman who works in a shop stands at that counter four days a week, and the eighteenth was not one of the four. **The woman who runs that shop told her on the Thursday, from the glass on the door, that a man with a bag propped the pipe and wired it and went before she had her coat on; said she is not going out to ask him and will sell him a roll of paper like anybody else; and said that a prop under a pipe changes none of what the autumn is going to cost.** He came in on the Saturday, was served, paid and left, has not been back, and is unaware that either of those two women spoke.

## 2. A sentence about a lamp, and the second room

- **MAJOR TURN: A THING THE MAN IN HIS SIXTIES HAS SAID BEFORE GOES ONE PLACE FURTHER WITHOUT EITHER MAN INTENDING IT, THE PERSON WHO HEARD IT IN THE FIRST PLACE HEARS THAT IT HAS LEFT THAT ROOM, AND NEITHER FACT HELPS ANYBODY.** In the hall on Carrow Lane, on a meter, on the evening of the twenty-second, the man in his sixty-something said that a lamp over a bank door was lit in broad daylight on the twentieth, and that a man and a woman with a clipboard stood in the road under it measuring it with a thumb before going back in, and that the lamp has been off both times he has looked since. **He said himself that he ought to have had it in another room.**
- The woman who works in a shop said one sentence: it has been in two rooms now, and it is about a bank in a town none of them has ever worked in. The man in his forties said a bank is a bank.
- In the room at the back of that parade's shop, the man who works nights asked the man in his thirties what the man in his sixties had been on about, and was given the whole of it, all of it, at second hand. **The woman who works in a shop put two questions to him — how many hours that lamp had been burning that afternoon, and whose lamp it was — and was given no answer to either of them. The man who works nights asked whether it was one of the four, and was told it is not, that nobody is setting it beside any of the four, and that nobody is saying it isn't.**
- The man in his forties came in and told the woman who works in a shop, in front of the man in his thirties, that the man in his sixties has already said a thing about a lamp in another room in this town, so it is in five places and nobody is going to any of them. **The man in his thirties heard, from a man in the same room, that his own sentence had already been said in a room he was still standing in.**
- **A HALL ON A METER IS NOT A COUNCIL. NOBODY PRODUCED A FIFTH THING, NONE OF THE FOUR WAS CORRECTED OR IMPROVED ON OR ADDED TO, THE FOURTH WAS NOT CALLED A FIFTH, AND THE TWENTY-EIGHTH OF JUNE WAS NOT MENTIONED. THE MAN WHO INVENTED THE FIRST OF THOSE FOUR ANSWERS IS NOT IN THAT ROOM AND IS NOT TOLD.**

## 3. A length of four inch with nobody on the end of it

- **MAJOR TURN: THE JOB STANDS WHERE IT IS, TWO PEOPLE DISAGREE, BOTH OF THEM HAVE A POINT, AND IT IS LEFT ON THAT LANDING WHERE THE DISAGREEMENT HAPPENED.** A drainage man capped nothing, so a length of open four inch came three foot out of a first-floor landing in a building off Cleeve Street with a hopper on the side of it. The man in his thirties cut the old stack off, got a new length up through the ceiling on a cord, and stood it on the step up into the top flat, one end on the step and the other about seven foot in the air.
- A coupling cannot be run onto a length standing in the air at the end of itself, and the joint has to come to the man or the pipe has to be held while he makes it. **The woman who works in a shop lives in the top flat, is the only person in that building that morning, and would not stand under it holding a pipe while somebody worked a caulk iron in the joint.**
- **Her side:** a hole in that ceiling now, a hopper going down four floors onto open pipe, a blockage in about six weeks, and a man who comes on two days a month who did the bottom of it and will do the top of it and it will be his own joint. **His side:** the same six weeks, but a fault made by a stranger on a step, a leak in a fortnight, nobody's name on any paper because there is nothing to put it on, and a trade he would be shown up in. He put the length down rather than do it badly.
- A man came up that stair with a folded trolley and a set of steps and moved a fridge out of the ground-floor flat over about four minutes. **Nobody asked him to hold anything and he did not offer.** Afterwards the man in his thirties said a man halfway up a stair with a fridge on a trolley on a working morning is not an arm anybody can borrow, and that he would have wanted paying for the four minutes in a way there was nothing to write it on.
- What is left on that landing: a capped stack, a five-inch hole in the ceiling with old plaster round it, and the tools and offcuts of that unfinished job still against the wall of that landing. **A trolley is not an arrangement and a man with a trolley is not a crew, a ladder is not a rescue, nobody gave anybody permission in that chapter, and nobody did.**

## 4. One cafe, described twice, the subjects the wrong way round

- **MAJOR TURN: TWO PEOPLE DESCRIBE THE SAME CHANGE TO THE SAME PARADE TO STRANGERS INSIDE A QUARTER OF AN HOUR AND A QUARTER OF A MILE, IT IS NOT THE SAME CHANGE, AND NOBODY PUTS THEM TOGETHER.** At a pole-and-plate stop at the end of Marsh Lane with no shelter, a man in a fleece told a man with a dog that the shop that shuts early is going to be a cafe, that a man from the city has been looking at it for somebody, that he is after both shops, and that the radio man has until about August. **He gave his own standing as the fourth remove of it and said it was not his and he was not having it.**
- About a quarter of an hour later, with that shop's shutter hooked up to its top bar, the man who puts shutters up told a young man with a rucksack that the woman who runs the shop that shuts early has bought the radio shop, that the radio man is out by the end of June, and that she is doing the parade as one — two removes, off his fitter and the fitter's brother-in-law.
- **What the shutterman said that matters:** the two shutters came off the same bar in the same week, so whoever buys anything buys a parcel; nobody who sells radios is going to be the one that buys a cafe, and nobody behind a counter that shuts at two is going to end up running a radio shop; therefore one of the two men has it backwards, and it is not the one whose version is more interesting.
- He carried a ladder up the parade, knocked on the radio man's shutter and said the bar had dropped a half inch and he would shim it on his next day. **Nobody went into that shop, nobody knocked on anybody's door, nobody telephoned anybody, that parade got no paper at all in that week or in the one after, the shop came up on the Monday at its own time and shut at two, and nothing in that chapter was found and nothing in it was discovered.**

## 5. Three mornings with no water, and a tread screwed down

- **MAJOR TURN: THE BUILDING TAKES THE WATER OFF FOR THREE DAYS BY BEING PUT BACK ON AT THE WRONG END OF THE WEEK, SHE IS STILL IN THAT ROOM ON THE SAME TERMS, SOMEBODY ELSE SCREWS A TREAD DOWN, AND THE TWO ARE NOT CONNECTED.** The stop tap is behind a board inside a case on the ground floor and is shut from the last evening of the week until somebody opens it in the morning. On the last Saturday she went down at half past seven and the handle was across, the board was off, the case door was standing open and the strip fitting was lit, which is itself unexplained.
- Three mornings, two empty jugs each time, and the cold tap at the back of the ground floor flat giving about two seconds and stopping. **The meter on that passage wall ran for those three days and was the one thing in the whole building that got what it wanted, and nobody living there has ever taken its reading.** She put the board back, shut the case, carried the jugs up, and said nothing to anybody, then or since, and did not put it in a letter.
- In the middle of those days the woman next door along came out of her door, could see two jugs and an unplugged kettle through a door open about two inches, and offered half a bucket of her own washing-up water for the kettle and for washing, on the terms that nothing was said about it and nobody fetched it. **She was refused, the door shut, and the woman next door along has not set foot on that landing again since, and has told nobody anything about those three days.**
- The water came back on the Tuesday morning and she filled both jugs and did not find out who had put the tap across on the Friday night. **She heard somebody on that landing at about half past ten that night and did not open her door and has said nothing about it.**
- **The other thing.** The second tread up the outside stair lifts half an inch and knocks once. On the Thursday the man in his forties took the loose corner up, found one screw sheared and one pulled out with a plug the size of a finger, drilled the hole out, put a longer screw through the bracket into sound concrete, and put his weight on it twice. It did not move and made no noise. **He went back down that stair, did not knock on a single door, and three feet below the woman of about fifty-five she went up past him with both jugs full, and not one word passed between them.**
- **A LADDER IS NOT A RESCUE, A GIFT IS NOT A PURCHASE, A PUMP IS NOT A RESCUE, AND THE WOMAN OF ABOUT FIFTY-FIVE IS NOT HOUSED BY ANYBODY IN THIS CHAPTER. Nobody arranged the door of her room, nobody asked her about it, nobody told her who fitted the hasp on the inside of it, and nobody in that building has ever spoken to her about any part of that door. The brass cap is still on that outlet. The blanket is still across the foot of her bed. The tread does not knock. The two are not connected.**

## 6. One frame in April, one in May, and nobody who saw both

- **MAJOR TURN: THE THING IS LEFT TWICE BY TWO PEOPLE WHO HAVE NEVER MET AND WHO NEVER FIND OUT ABOUT EACH OTHER, AND NEITHER OF THEM IS EVER ASKED.** The object is a folded metal greenhouse frame in three sections, six foot by two foot out, folding to six foot by a foot by four inches, painted steel, the weight of a man, with a bent corner on the second section.
- **First, in the first week of April,** a woman leaned one against the brick of a yard at the top of a lane behind the parade, having got it there in three goes on a barrow because she could not carry two at once, on its long edge with the bent corner out of the weather. Her neighbour's gate was shut, the next gate was shut, both households either side of hers were empty all day, and the only man who came the length of that lane with a barrow that day walked past a woman standing a bundle of pipe against a wall without stopping. **Nobody asked her a single question about it, in any direction, and she has not walked that lane again since.**
- It has been there five weeks, with about three weeks of weather on it and green along the bottom, where that edge has been standing in standing water since the winter. The man with the barrow has walked by it five times without stopping once, and it is nobody's and it is not in the road, so there is nothing there to ask anybody about.
- **Second, on the tenth of May,** the man in his thirties put one against brick in the yard two doors above a shop on that lane, and would not put it on the roof rack because of a wind coming up off the back of the parade. He told the woman who works in a shop, from the alley, that it is nobody's, is not the shop's, is not his, will be there a few days and then go, and that the honest answer is that he knows of four in this town and two are up a garden and one is a bench in a garage and one is against a wall further up the same lane. **She did not go and look, and had not been near that yard since the week before, and if she had she would not have joined the two together.**
- He had forgotten the first one, and it was still in that yard both times he went back. **No single person in this borough saw both. A copy is not a wall and an envelope is not a tray, and a thing that is found is not a discovery, and nothing in that chapter was found.**

## 7. A fortnight said on a pavement, and refused on paper

- **MAJOR TURN: HE SAYS WHAT A JOB COSTS IN FORTNIGHTS AND NOT THAT HE WILL DO IT, TWO PEOPLE HEAR IT AS A PROMISE, HE DENIES IT IN A SHOP, AND THE SLATES ARE STILL ON THE GABLE.**
- **WHAT IS DIFFERENT FROM 0311, WHICH IS WHAT THE CARD REQUIRED BE NAMED: the work is two slates on a gable over a bay window and not a step in a road · the person who brings it back to him is the woman those two slates are over, and not a witness with a tally · she is not a professional rememberer, she is a person asking whether a thing is going to fall on her, and she says so · nothing happens at the trade counter at all, which is where 0311's reckoning was · there is no bus stop and no open end of a hard · and the pad is on the boards of his van and is not brought in and is not opened, where in 0311 it went onto a hall table face up for a second. **AND THE ENGINE IS INVERTED: 0311 HUNG ON NOBODY HAVING ASKED HIM, AND HERE SOMEBODY ASKS HIM, WHICH IS THE FIRST TIME IN THIS ACCOUNT THAT ANYBODY HAS.**
- Two slates on that gable have slipped about two inches, are still on rusted nails, and have their nail heads standing about half an inch proud. One of them is the size of a door, and there is a woman in that bay with a table and a plant on it most days. On the morning of the fifth of May, at about half past eight, he told a man in a fleece what that job wants: a tower, somebody to build it, somebody else to hold the boards, a day and a half of a weekend, his own yard to get the tower out of, and no scaffold over the front of a bay in the dark or in a wind. **He said a fortnight and said in the same breath that nobody in that house had asked him.**
- What the man in a fleece heard was a man telling him when it will be done. What the woman in the bay heard, from that same man at her own door four days later, was a fortnight and a man who turns up, and she has said from the outset that she only has his word for it. **His answer to her is arithmetic and not a denial of having spoken: he says how long things take, and that if she had come out of her door on the fifth of May he would have asked her for a telephone number and put her street in that pad, and did not, and is not going to now. His answer to the piece of paper she wants is the one about there being nothing in this town to put a name to, and to that he adds a second reason which is the real one: written down, a slate through her window puts him in it, and unwritten it does not.**
- She wanted him to say he would not do it, so that she could say she heard him say that, and she wanted it on something. **He said there is nothing to write it on, that nobody has given him anything in this town to put his name on, and that a paper with his name at the top would in six months be the only sheet in this borough carrying his name and it would be about two slates. He did not open the pad. Nobody asked him for a copy and nobody has one.**
- The man in a fleece said he had said one man's business to another man on a pavement and had never meant it for a woman who counts things for a living. **The slates are on the gable and the nails still stand half an inch proud of them both, and in a year or two either a gale in the autumn takes them off that pitch, or a man with a tower is up there for some other job and refixes them, and he will have had nothing to do with either.**

## 8. A machine taken in, used once, put in a corner

- **MAJOR TURN: THE THING IS TAKEN IN AND USED ONCE AND PUT SOMEWHERE ELSE, THE PERSON WHO TOOK IT IN IS NOT THE PERSON IT WAS FOR, AND HE NEVER FOUND OUT.** It is a good upright vacuum cleaner with a lead of about fifteen foot and the cuff at the machine end of it cracked, on the boards of the man with the van since the second week of April, out of a house on the Cleeve road emptied for a woman now living a long way off, out of a cupboard under that house's stairwell, the filter holding somebody's hair, taken because the woman from the letting agency said take it in a yard with the loader running. **It is not a saucepan and nothing in it came out of a skip.**
- **Everything he knows about the woman of about fifty-five is a single thing:** in the third week of January he was in the yard at the back of that building, late on a weekday afternoon, with a torch in his hand on a pipe, and a light was on in a first-floor front room in broad daylight, and he has thought about that light since January. He said all of that at the bottom step and then said she could say no.
- The woman who works in a shop told him there is one person on that floor at the end of the corridor, said she was not going up with him and was not telling him anything about her, and said she would open the street door and take an end of it because that stair gives a person barely three feet the whole way up — **and said plainly that she was doing it for the width of that stair and for nothing else.**
- She told him the machine was going up or going back down, and told him to leave it on the landing rather than bring it into her room, which measures about nine foot each way and already holds a bed, a chair and a table, and has nowhere in it for anything the size of a second chair. He put it against that wall with the handle up and came back down, and the three of them ended up in the street, and she went back up and shut her own door. **He raised a hand from the cab two days later and she raised one, and that was all of it, and he has not gone up that street again.**
- **Used once, on a Sunday**, on about twenty feet of concrete, brush off and the tube on the cracked cuff, because there is sand on that landing at the junction of the concrete and the skirtings, along the fire door, and in the the gap under the top flat's door, and a broom gets into none of those places. The lead is fifteen foot and the landing is twenty-six, so it was done in two goes and the plug moved. **The filter came up with about half a bag of grit in it, that noise brought the woman in the room next door along out of her door, and she said aye, well, and went back in.** Then it went over on its side in the corner of that landing where it widens at the fire door, the lead on top of it, against a wall that is not straight, and it has not been run again and the filter has not been emptied.
- **A gift that is not refused is not a purchase, a person who is not counted is not a person who is helped, being let in is not a rescue, and being turned away is not a falling-out. The two of them were entirely civil, nobody turned anybody away, and the machine is a machine.**

## 9. One day, five rooms, and not one task put to him

- **MAJOR TURN: HE IS GIVEN NO TASK BY ANYBODY IN ANY OF FIVE ROOMS ON ONE DAY, FOUR OF THOSE FIVE ROOMS HAVE NO WORK IN THEM FOR HIM AT ALL, HE SAYS SOMETHING IN A CORRIDOR THAT NOBODY ANSWERS, AND HE WALKS HOME.**
- **WHAT IS DIFFERENT FROM 0313, WHICH IS WHAT THE CARD REQUIRED BE NAMED: one day and not six · **ONLY TWO OF THE FIVE PLACES ARE NEW, BEING A GROCERY DELIVERY PUT ON THE FLOOR INSTEAD OF A GROUND-FLOOR LAY WHEREBACK CHAIR WAS GIVEN TO HIM BY NAME, AND A HOUSE AT THE END OF A LANE INSTEAD OF A KITCHEN IN A HOUSE UP ONE**; THE BACK OF A PARADE, THE CORRIDOR BY THE WATER AND THE HALL OFF THE PARADE ARE 0313'S OWN THREE, AND AN EARLIER PASS DESCRIBED ALL FIVE AS NEW, WHICH WAS WRONG **· not one instruction and not one word, where 0313 had a woman say two words and give him a time · and no bus, and he walks two miles.**
- The kitchen was clean: surfaces wiped and dry, both hobs off, the sink through a cycle, the rack over it empty, the board at its end empty, and no third glass anywhere in it, and the man who cooks in it had gone home at about nine and left no note. He washed a wooden spoon and a knife, wiped the sink, and stood in that kitchen. **Nobody came in, and the only way into it is through that shop, which was shut for another three quarters of an hour.**
- The greengrocer's: a delivery was due and the woman who runs that shop said two words to a man who was not him. It came at about ten past twelve, six boxes and a sack on the floor in the middle of that shop, and **not one of them was put anywhere near him.** She got one sack up onto her own shoulder and shoved the other two into the cold room with her foot. He was told to have a good morning at about ten past twelve and that was the whole of it.
- The house at the end of Marsh Lane: the man was out, the woman was not back until about eight, and nobody there knew he was in the house, there was no work, he made one cup of tea out of a tin that is not his, sat at that table for half an hour, did not go upstairs and did not look in the cupboards.
- The corridor: the tap in that building is shut from the last evening of the week until somebody opens it, and he was in the passage at about eight. **There was nothing on that floor to mop, and he went along it with the bucket anyway and then stood at the top of that stair, on the outside, for about ten minutes.** Just before midday she came out of the far room with the two empty jugs, went down, and shut that street door at the bottom; at about ten past twelve she came back up with both full and a kettle hanging off the arm that had no jug on it.
- **What he said, to her back as she went through her own door, not loudly:** that he is in that building about eight on a Monday and she is on that stair at about ten past seven with the two empty ones, so he has seen her and she has not seen him, and he was not going to stand up there and get her to swing round on a landing with a jug in each hand and say anything about it. **She said nothing. The door shut. The woman next door along, whose own door never shuts further than the width of a hand, said nothing either, and neither of them has said one word to anybody about anything since.**
- The hall: **he stopped being on the tea in that hall before Christmas and has not been back on it, nobody in that room has said so to him, and he has not asked anybody there either.** He got himself in at about half past six; the urn was already on and already hot; two cups were drying on a cloth; the table was set for four, and he was not one of the four. **Nobody said one word to him, and at ten to seven the man in his thirties put his usual thing on the table, and nobody in that room took any notice of it in front of the lad.** He left at about a quarter to seven and walked the two miles, offered nothing and was offered nothing, having gone back for his own mop and bucket at half past four and left the pair of them in a bin store at the head of that parade.
- **He is on the sink. HE HAS NO TITLE, GRADE, DEPARTMENT, CHAIR, SEAT, MEMBERSHIP, FORM OR ROTA ROW, BEING ASKED IS NOT A STANDING, BEING USEFUL IS NOT A JOB AND BEING IN A ROOM IS NOT BEING A MEMBER, THE QUESTION HE ASKED ON THE FOURTH OF JUNE 2016 WAS NOT PUT TO ANYBODY IN THAT ROOM AND WAS NOT ANSWED BY ANYBODY IN IT, AND THE FIRE BLANKET WAS NOT MENTIONED TO THE WOMAN IN THE ROOM NEXT DOOR ALONG.** A lad in five rooms is not a crew, a day with no question in it is a device and not a finding, and no chapter of this batch contains a thanks of any kind.

## 10. A borough, a Saturday, and a gutter with birds in it

- **THE REGISTER IS AT NINETEEN, THE LINE UNDER IT IS NINE HUNDRED AND THIRTEEN DAYS AND IS STILL BLANK, AND THAT IS THE ONE DAY-COUNT IN THE PROSE OF THESE TEN CHAPTERS.** It sits on the second shelf up in a bay off a first-floor corridor in the building by the water, behind a door that never shuts over its catch and was left standing open behind two women at about half past ten. The last thing in it is a lamp belonging to a yard, and somebody went the round of the district and entered that bulb as gone in about November two years ago under a heading reserved for that sort of thing, and nothing has been written under that reserved heading since. Below that line the sheet goes on a couple of fingers and then gives up about two inches short of the lower edge of the book, and nobody left that gap; the book has it. **The two women were after a chair out of a corridor being painted, took the one at the end of that corridor, carried it out between them more than six foot away from that bay, and had no idea what was on the shelf above them.**
- **and the borough is ordinary and the month is a month and not a turn of anything:** the first dry morning since before Christmas · about nine birds along the lip of a gutter in a row facing the same way, gone over the roof tops at about nine in about four minutes · a greengrocer's shutter up with a man inside it on a step · the shop that shuts early down since two o'clock on the Thursday · a plate of two sets on a board on a pavement with marker prices on small cards gone soft in the sun · a hand of gravel and a dry twig in the bin for gritting and a hole in one side of it still the size of a closed hand · a unlettered white van at the top of Marsh Lane on all but about two days in ten · two slates on a gable with their nail heads half an inch proud, a woman in that bay behind her own table, with a plant on it, and nobody on that pavement looking up · and a vacuum cleaner lying over on its side in the corner of that landing where it widens at the fire door, with roughly half a bag of sand out of a corner in it.
- **AND FOUR ANSWERS ARE STILL GOING ROUND THAT TABLE AND NONE OF THEM CAN BE PUT BESIDE ANY OTHER.** Four of them have been on that table since the twelfth of January, the four lying there that evening were the same four, and no one in this borough has been able to put one of them next to another. The woman who works in a shop gave the count of the four and the count of the things nobody has done about them, which is that none has been added, none removed, and no origin has been given for any of them, and then said she is not going to sit there totting up four things whose edges she has never laid a hand on. **The man in his forties said that if four are going to turn into something other than four that evening it will not be by anybody at that table deciding to count, and that somebody who turns four into five simply to see what five would look like has found nothing and has only handed the rest of them more work. The man in his sixties then asked outright what the four amount to between them, and nobody at that table answered him at all, and a chair went back against the wall.**
- **THE QUESTION IS STILL A QUESTION, AND IT IS A DIFFERENT WORDING FROM 0299, WHICH IS THE CHAPTER THAT HAS HIM ASK IT.** The man from the parade put his cup down near the edge of that table and said he was not putting it beside those four, and asked whether in this town a thing that was done and a thing somebody has put on paper afterwards are two different kinds of thing at all, on the grounds that he has been in a room for each, that the version on paper came out better than the thing itself, and that nobody in either room has ever put the two side by side, and he would like somebody there to tell him whether it can run the other way. **The man in his forties said that is a question, the man from the parade agreed that it is, the man in his forties said then it stays one, and the table let it lie.**
- The man in his thirties raised the two slates on that corner gable and the nails standing out of them, and the man in his forties had that from a tradesman, and declined to develop it beyond that. The gas went out at nine, the case was shut and locked, the chairs went up, the porch light came on with the door and was still burning a quarter of an hour afterwards, when the lamp at the corner outside took over from it. That gas has come out of one woman's own pocket since the summer and from nowhere else.
- **NOBODY IS RESCUED. NOBODY DIES. NOBODY RENEWS ANYTHING. NOTHING WAS MINUTED. NOBODY WAS THANKED AND NOBODY REFUSED TO BE THANKED. THE ELEVEN STANDING REFUSALS ARE ELEVEN. CARD 0324 IS NOT A CLOSE AND IS NOT CALLED ONE.**

EVERY DAY-COUNT BELOW IS CALCULATED FROM ITS ANCHOR ON THE DAY IN QUESTION AND NOT CARRIED FORWARD FROM A SUMMARY: at Saturday 8 July 2017 the blank line under the nineteenth is nine hundred and fifty-five days, the card in the slot is eight hundred and sixty-nine, and the letter in the tray is eight hundred and seventy-one.**

## 1. A van across a gate, twice, a month apart

- **MAJOR TURN: THE SAME KIND OF INTERRUPTION HAPPENS TWICE, A MONTH OR MORE ON, TO TWO DIFFERENT PEOPLE, NEITHER FINDS OUT ABOUT THE OTHER, AND NEITHER WOULD HAVE SAID ANYTHING IF THEY HAD.** On Sunday 28 May a white van with nothing on it stands across the gate of a yard behind the sixth of nine shops on a parade, and the man in his thirties cannot get his own van out, waits about an hour and a half, moves his own out by inches, and does the remainder of that Sunday on his feet.
- He is not on anybody's list in that street and nobody in that parade knows a van was ever in that passage. **The man who puts shutters up does not work Sundays, and that is the whole of the reason nobody came.**
- **The second time, on Thursday 29 June, it is the back gate of the shop where the woman who works in a shop is standing**, and it has gone in nose first and turned round, and the man with a trolley cannot get his front wheels through. **She will not have six boxes put down on a pavement with about nine inches of it, so he carries them in on a sheet of his own in two goes and she points at the two heavy ones.** She then puts a hand flat on a warm van, shuts a gate that latches, with a drop bolt on the inside face, and has not been in that yard since except for her bin.
- A man in a fleece tells her she would not be the first and that a tipper covers two gates at once once a week and never knows. **She says she is not going to knock on anybody's door and not going to take up position in that alley watching every vehicle that enters, because the first one she stopped she would have to stop the next, and within a month she would be that passage's obstruction, with no afterwards of being anybody else.**
- **Neither was ever told about the other and neither asked a question. A person who is not counted is not a person who is helped and nothing here comes near refuting it.**

## 2. A firm nine miles off, and a question nobody answered

- **MAJOR TURN: THE SENTENCE GETS FURTHER AND THE ROOM IT REACHES HAS A PERSON IN IT WHO WAS NOT IN THE FIRST ROOM AND WHO ASKS ONE QUESTION, AND IT IS NOT ANSWERED.** In the hall on Carrow Lane, on a meter, the man in his sixties reports a firm down the estuary that does the outsides of houses and roofs and has gone over, and that you cannot get anybody.
- **He has not got the name of it and the man at the works had not either; the name of it had been painted on a vehicle that has since gone.** The man in his forties will not let it be counted: a thing heard twice is not a thing checked, is not a smaller version of a thing checked and is not a second of the same — it is the same size and no heavier. It is not one of the four, nobody is setting it beside those four, and nobody is going to say the number has gone up.
- **The woman who works in a shop sat through all of that in the hall and carries it into the room at the back of her shop in her own words, and the man who works nights asks which firm, and she has not got it, and says that going back to the hall to ask a man of that age to say where it came from would stop the sentence there.**
- **The other thing that morning is not connected to any of it.** A man arrives with an under-counter fridge on a hand truck and it will not go through a back door with a steel bar on the outside. The man who works nights says take the bar off; she will not, because two houses behind that yard had their doors off their hinges twice in a fortnight in March; the man with the hand truck will not stand in a doorway with a back door open on an alley. The bar comes off, the fridge goes in, and **the fridge is then inside the swing of a door that will not close against it, so the bar cannot be hooked back on and its key is in an office in a town that shut in the autumn, and it stays off, and nobody will be able to say why.**
- **A HALL ON A METER IS NOT A COUNCIL. NO FIFTH OF ANYTHING WAS PRODUCED, THE THIRD ANSWER WAS NOT CORRECTED AND NOT ADDED TO, THE FOURTH WAS NOT CALLED A FIFTH, THE TWENTY-EIGHTH OF JUNE WAS NOT MENTIONED, NOBODY WENT TO LOOK AT ANYTHING, AND NOBODY FOUND A DOCUMENT.**

## 3. Two jobs on one landing, both standing

- **MAJOR TURN: THE THING STANDS, THE DISAGREEMENT IS WITH SOMEBODY WHO WAS NOT IN THE LAST ONE, BOTH HAVE A POINT, AND IT IS LEFT WHERE THE DISAGREEMENT HAPPENED AND NOBODY WILL EVER KNOW HOW OFTEN THAT HAS HAPPENED ON THAT LANDING.** A new sash in a first-floor landing will not go up: painted shut about four times, the top rail swollen an eighth of an inch into the outer stop, and pushing it takes the top corner out of its rebate. **It is a two-man job and the man in his thirties says so twice, on a landing, because somebody has to hold the head of it while somebody works the bottom rail.**
- **The man in the ground floor flat has held the head of a sash for him three times and is the only man he can think of who would.** He comes out at about ten in his socks and says, before anything is asked, that it is a two-man job, that the man has not got anybody, that he is on at four, and that the trestle is across his own front door. **His last point is the one that is conceded: By saying nothing you have enrolled me in it.**
- The second job is the same morning. A woman in the middle flat took her own front door off its hinges on the Sunday to get a wardrobe up and cannot get it back; the top left screw has gone into air because that corner of the frame has dropped half an inch. **He can pack the corner alone; he cannot steady a door against a closing frame while he takes the slack out of the same hinge. She says in one sentence that she is not standing in her own hallway holding a door while somebody works a chisel inches from her hand, and he tells her she is not the second pair of hands, that the man downstairs is.**
- Both stand, the man downstairs is not asked a second time, and **the only person told any of it is the woman who works in a shop, who asks how many and is told that nobody has ever counted anything in this town and no one has ever been given a pencil on a landing and asked to write, and that it could be dozens or it could be four.**
- **A TROLLEY IS NOT AN ARRANGEMENT, A LADDER IS NOT A RESCUE, A LADDER IS NOT A CREW, AND NOBODY GAVE ANYBODY PERMISSION IN THAT CHAPTER AND NOBODY DID.**

## 4. Two wrong versions of a collection day

- **MAJOR TURN: THE TWO THINGS CANNOT BOTH BE TRUE, THE ONLY PERSON WHO COULD PUT THEM TOGETHER IS NOT IN EITHER PLACE, AND A THIRD PERSON HEARS BOTH AND SAYS NEITHER OF THEM IS NOISE.** At a stop at the bottom of the Cleeve road the man in a fleece tells a stranger that the Thursday is moving, that this parade is going onto a different day from the middle of the month, and that it is the road works. **The man who puts shutters up tells a woman outside a closed shop that it is the Tuesday, and that it is not one parade but two hundred yard of the top of the road, because if one street moves and the next does not you have a street where nothing comes.**
- **One claims a parade and one claims a stretch of road, and the two are about eight hundred yard apart, and only one of the two men was in the road.**
- **The notice that would settle it went on about sixty doors from the Thursday, off a lorry carrying no lettering at all, four men, knocking and putting it on anyway, about three quarters of an hour.** Nobody in the shop and nobody at the stop saw a man get out of that lorry; the shutterman was two hundred yard off and the man in a fleece was at work. **The shutterman put his in his pocket without reading it and binned it the same night, and the man who works in the shop tore his off because it was under the foot of his ladder and binned it with the paper. Nobody in that parade has read one.**
- **Her conclusion: that is not noise, and it is not two things either. It is the weather, and the only thing that could tell them which is right is on sixty doors nobody has read.** A copy is not a wall, an envelope is not a tray, and a thing that is found is not a discovery; nothing in that chapter was found.

## 5. A fifth thing a building did, and a bin on two studs

- **MAJOR TURN: THE BUILDING DOES A FIFTH THING, SHE IS STILL IN THAT ROOM ON THE SAME TERMS, SOMEBODY ELSE FIXES SOMETHING ELSE, AND THE TWO FIXES ARE NOT CONNECTED BY ANYBODY.** A glazed lantern on a flat roof over that first-floor corridor has nothing sealing round the exposed outside edge of its curb, and water comes down the wall of the stairwell, along the underside of the ceiling, over the head of two doors, and off a wall in a crescent the height of a chair back, taking the paper off the skirting in one strip about two foot long.
- **Nobody has been on that roof, nobody has opened that landing cupboard, and nobody has had a ladder out of the back of that building in June.** The woman of about fifty-five found the water on the Tuesday of that week and set her own washing-up bowl under the strip with her own hands. She carries it out several times a day, has a second bowl for the jugs and a towel round the first one, and there is a wet mark on the ceiling the size of a tea tray that has not got bigger at all since the bowl went under. **She is still in that room on the same terms and nobody arranged anything for her and nobody asked her about it.**
- **The woman in the room next door along came out on the Wednesday, saw the bowl, said there was a smell off that wall in the morning, and shut her door. She added that the caretaker does nothing in that building between about eight and about six, and then made clear the remark was for the woman beside her, not for the corridor, and has not been out on that landing since.** Nothing was offered and nothing was refused, which is not what happened here in May.
- **Three streets off, on the Friday morning, the man in his forties found a wheelie bin in the middle of a passage on its side with a puddle under it.** It hooks over two studs and the lower one had a quarter of an inch of play. **He lifted it, set it on its brackets, fetched a key, took up the slack, and pulled: the whole thing came free of the wall in one movement, as designed. He did not listen at it, did not look inside it, and knocked on neither of the two front doors.**
- **She tells him that a deed and a credit for it are not the same weight here, and that she has watched one of the second turn into the first inside a month.** He came back with a length of angle the following week and neither of those two houses was in. **The two repairs are four hundred yard apart and no living person connects them.**
- **A LADDER IS NOT A RESCUE, A GIFT IS NOT A PURCHASE, A PUMP IS NOT A RESCUE, AND THE WOMAN OF ABOUT FIFTY-FIVE IS NOT HOUSED BY ANYBODY IN THIS CHAPTER.**

## 6. Three of one thing in a lane, and a count of three

- **MAJOR TURN: THE THING IS LEFT THREE TIMES, THE PEOPLE WHO LEFT IT DO NOT KNOW OF EACH OTHER, THE ONLY PERSON WHO KNOWS ANYTHING IS SOMEBODY WHO WAS NOT THERE FOR ANY OF IT, AND BY THE TIME ANYBODY WOULD ASK, THE LAST ONE HAS BEEN TAKEN AWAY BY SOMEBODY WHO WAS NOT ASKED EITHER.** The object is a painted timber gate, four foot by three, white, shipped in a polythene sleeve with brown tape at its waist, and **the sleeve gives no clue at all to its age.**
- **First, in the third week of May, in a yard near the top of that lane; he saw it from the lane because he had to walk past the gate to reach a side gate, and it was there on the Thursday and the Friday after. Second, in the first week of June, further up from the shop, in the yard that gets the afternoon; he saw it while in there ten minutes over a fitting that did not need replacing, and told the woman who works in a shop in four sentences from the alley and she did not go and look. Third, on Monday 19 June, in the yard by the car park, seen at about four in the afternoon.**
- **He counted them on the Wednesday morning by walking the lane, and the number is three, and it is the first time he has said a number like it out loud to anybody.** The first and the third are gone — a green mark on a gable wall, a wet rectangle and a scuff of white and a scrap of wrapper on a kerbstone. The second is still there, gone a dirty grey.
- **THE REVERSAL IS MARKED ON THE PAGE AND NOT LEFT SILENT, BECAUSE 0327 HAD HIM REFUSING A COUNT IN THAT SAME SHOP DOORWAY A FORTNIGHT AND A DAY EARLIER: he put it to her then that he had never counted anything in this town, and that the day anybody started counting was the day a thing like that stopped being a thing a man kept to himself, and on this lane he works out that the two halves of that sentence are not the same half, that a man can keep one and lose the other, and that what he has lost it on is saying a number out loud where a person is standing in the room with him.** A reader who does not see that paragraph will read 0330 as an error.
- **He gets the pad out in the van and shuts it again, because a pad carrying a number has no home in this borough and a sheet in a van listing three items is a sheet that will be mislaid.**
- **The only person who watched one of them go is the woman who works in a shop, from her own window, at about six in the morning on the Tuesday, and she told nobody and does not know the man with the barrow's name, and he found out only after he had counted three, and did not ask her to say she had told him.** He corrected her once about a scrap of wrapper and she was right: a scrap of the wrapper is not a gate.

## 7. A promise he does not deny making

- **MAJOR TURN: HE SAYS HE WILL DO IT AND HE DOES NOT DO IT AND SOMEBODY ELSE SAYS HE SAID HE WOULD, AND HE DOES NOT DENY SAYING IT, AND ALL THREE OF THEM ARE BEING STRAIGHT.** **THIS IS THE THIRD TIME ON THE PAGE AND NOT THE SECOND: 0311 WAS A STEP WITH A WOMAN KEEPING A FOOTFALL TALLY, 0321 WAS TWO SLATES AND A FORTNIGHT SAID IN A ROAD AND A REQUEST FOR A PIECE OF PAPER REFUSED TWICE, AND BOTH OF THOSE ENDED ON A DENIAL. 0331 ENDS ON AN ADMISSION.**
- The thing is three lengths of close-board lying against the brick of a yard, out of a job, not put up. the opening measures about four foot across shoulder height, and about nine people went through it on that Sunday morning.
- **What he said, on the Friday before, at a door, to a man in a fleece on the step: that he would close that opening before the month is out.** The reason, given nine days later in about four seconds in a road, is that **the mistake was made off the doorstep rather than in the yard, and a promise made about another person's ground is a promise about their ground.**
- **The woman with a clipboard has that gate on her sheet twice with two different numbers on it and knows it is one gate, and a man in a fleece told her a man had said he would have it closed before the end of the month. She does not say he did it. He gives it to her straight, at that door, nine days ago, and will not be the one that was misheard.**
- She asks whether he will do it and **he says no, rules out the two easy reasons first, gives the real one, and then says the thing that puts it out of reach of being a conversation: no third party exists who could have heard it, him and a man in a fleece, and that man is not here.** **She writes about a quarter of an inch on the edge of her sheet, looks at it, rubs it out with a thumb, and says nothing about having done it. She says there is not a column for this and she is not starting one on a Sunday morning in June for a man who told her straight.**
- **The pad comes out of the box in the road and is shut again, and the other reason is given: a line of that street in that pad would be the only record in the borough of a promise about a boundary, and it would live in a van. On the second of July the three lengths are still against that wall and the gap is still four foot wide.**
- **A LIST IS NOT A DOCUMENT, A LIST IS NOT A FORM, THERE IS NO FORM IN THIS BOROUGH, A THING SAID OUT LOUD IS NOT A THING ANYBODY KEEPS, AND NOBODY IN THAT CHAPTER WAS GIVEN A TITLE, A GRADE, A DEPARTMENT, A CHAIR, A SEAT, A MEMBERSHIP, A ROW OR A WAY OF BEING THANKED.**

## 8. A carpet laid once and a chest refused

- **MAJOR TURN: ONE THING IS TAKEN IN AND USED ONCE AND PUT SOMEWHERE ELSE, A SECOND THING IS HELD OUT IN FRONT OF THE SAME DOOR AND REFUSED, THE PERSON WHO REFUSES IT DOES NOT EXPLAIN AND IS NOT ASKED, AND THE PERSON WHO BROUGHT THE FIRST ONE NEVER HEARD ABOUT EITHER.** **It is not a vacuum cleaner, it is not a saucepan, and nothing in it came out of a skip or a yard.**
- The carpet is off a roll, about nine foot by six, wool, dark, heavier than one man and lighter than two, with the underlay still pinned to it in staples. It came out of a front room on the Cleeve road in May and went up that outside stair in the second week of June with the woman of about fifty-five on the lead end, because it is a single and a half and it goes round the corner at the fire door. **She had said, before it came up, that no carpet was going in that room, and no carpet went in that room.**
- **She laid it herself on the Sunday, staples in, and on Friday 23 June she got a corner up off the skirting with her fingernails, and it was deformed by being lifted and no longer fitted, and on the Friday morning of the thirtieth she took it in hand once more and stood it edgeways where the wall takes the light. It is there now, a foot across the edge and reaching most of the way to the ceiling that wall.** a floor covering in that room would push every other thing against the walls and she will not arrange her life around a gift of that kind.
- **The same man came up that stair on the thirtieth with a chest** — about two foot by a foot by a foot, light wood, never painted, a lid and a catch. He said the whole of what he came to say and then said she could say no and that if she said no he would take it back down and shut the door. **She said no. He said right you are and went down with it and shut the street door and put it under a folding stool and two trestles in the back of his van. Four seconds passed between her no and him turning round and nothing was said in them by either person, and neither has said a word about it since. He did not ask.**
- **AND THE MACHINE IS NOT IN THAT CHAPTER AND NOTHING IN THAT CHAPTER SAYS IT WENT ANYWHERE, WHICH IS A FINDING AGAINST THIS BLOCK AND NOT AGAINST THE CHAPTER: 0332 IS A CARPET, A CHEST AND A REFUSAL, ITS CARD SAID THE SECOND THING MAY NOT BE A VACUUM CLEANER, AND THE WORD APPEARS NOWHERE IN IT. THE LAST THING THE PAGE SAYS ABOUT THE MACHINE IS 0329, WHERE IT IS STILL LYING OVER ON ITS SIDE IN THAT CORNER, AND ITS PRESENT STATE IS: A good upright with a fifteen-foot lead and a cracked cuff at the machine end, taken up an outside stair in May and left on a first-floor landing, run once along twenty feet of concrete by a woman it was not for, and lying over on its side ever since in the corner where that landing widens by the fire door, against a wall that is not straight, its lead dropped across the machine, and the filter still holding roughly half a bag of grit off that same landing, and nobody has run it since and nobody has emptied it, AND IT STANDS ABOUT FIVE FOOT OFF THE BOWL, AND NEITHER OF THE TWO IS ANYTHING TO DO WITH THE OTHER.**
- **He gives the rule and the reason to the woman who works in a shop at a shop door: the correct length of time to refuse a man at your own step is about a second, and a person who stood there a minute would want to know why, and you do not obtain the reason, and a man who stands on that step a minute is a man who would have put a question to her on her own threshold about her refusal.** **A GIFT THAT IS NOT REFUSED IS NOT A PURCHASE, BEING LET IN IS NOT A RESCUE, AND BEING TURNED AWAY IS NOT A FALLING-OUT.**

## 9. One day, four rooms with somebody in them, and something said aloud

- **MAJOR TURN: NOBODY ASKS HIM ANYTHING AT ALL, HE SAYS SOMETHING ANYWAY IN FRONT OF OTHER PEOPLE, NOBODY ANSWERS IT, AND HE GOES HOME.** **WHAT IS DIFFERENT FROM 0313 AND 0323: 0313 WAS SIX DAYS AND A NUMBER AND 0323 HAD FOUR OF ITS FIVE ROOMS EMPTY OF PEOPLE, AND HERE IT IS ONE DAY AND FOUR OF THE FIVE ROOMS HAVE A PERSON IN THEM, AND HE CARRIES NOTHING INTO ANY OF THEM, AND WHAT HE SAYS IS NOT SAID TO A BACK.** It is said, across a table, not quietly, to a woman standing still who can hear, with two other people in the room.
- **AND THE DEVICE §11 OF THE 0013 BLOCK BELOW CALLED SPENT HAS NOW BEEN RUN A SEVENTH TIME, BECAUSE THE CARD FOR THIS CHAPTER ASKED FOR IT BY NAME. THAT IS A FLAG AND IT CARRIES FORWARD UNRESOLVED.**
- The kitchen at the back of the parade has the man who cooks in it in it, sitting with a cup and the radio on low, and three items waiting rather than two. **Nobody hands him anything and no destination is given him and he needs none, and at ten past eight the man puts his own cup in that sink and goes to work with the cloth still in his hand.**
- The greengrocer's: a man brings a sack of leeks in at twenty to twelve, sets it on the counter, says to the middle of the room that they are for the shop and on the account, and goes out. **The sack stands where he put it for two hours and nobody asked anybody to shift it.** The woman carried it into the cold room herself.
- The house at the end of Marsh Lane: two people say good morning and that is the whole of it; a cup is washed and put on its hook and washed again at about four and put back on the same hook.
- The corridor by the water: **there has been nothing on that floor to mop since before Christmas and he goes the length of his mop regardless.** The woman of about fifty-five goes down with two empty jugs at about a quarter to ten and comes back at ten past ten with both full and a kettle over her other arm. **He does not speak to her this time, and the reason is given plainly: he put a remark to her back in May, got no reply, the whole business stayed with him for a fortnight afterwards, and he had no intention of repeating it.**
- The hall off that parade: the woman of about forty-two holds that key, the urn had been on, two cups were drying, the table was set for four persons, and it offered him nothing without anyone informing him of that. **What he says at ten to seven, out loud, and what it is about: that the woman next door along in that building has not been seen in that corridor since a Wednesday nineteen days before, that she goes past that bowl twice a day, that she is the only other occupant of that landing, and that for seven months the pair have exchanged nothing whatever, and he has been there the whole time.** Nobody answers; the woman with the key says it is an unusual quantity of attention to have paid to two neighbours and the man in his forties says it is.
- **HE IS ON THE SINK. HE HAS NO TITLE, GRADE, DEPARTMENT, CHAIR, SEAT, MEMBERSHIP, ROW OR WRITTEN SHEET, BEING ASKED IS NOT A STANDING, BEING USEFUL IS NOT A JOB AND BEING IN A ROOM IS NOT BEING A MEMBER, THE QUESTION HE ASKED ON THE FOURTH OF JUNE 2016 WAS NOT PUT TO ANYBODY IN THAT ROOM AND WAS NOT ANSWERED BY ANYBODY IN IT, ITS NAME IS NOT GIVEN HERE AND IS NOT TO BE GIVEN, AND THE FIRE BLANKET WAS NOT MENTIONED TO THE WOMAN IN THE ROOM NEXT DOOR ALONG.** his bucket and mop were still in that store at half past four when he came back for them, and no resident of that building ever enters that store.

## 10. A borough, a Saturday in July, and nine people in a room

- **THE BOROUGH IS ORDINARY AND THE MONTH IS A MONTH AND NOT A TURN OF ANYTHING.** the warmest day of that year to date, and the tar outside the hall giving underfoot by early afternoon · a man on a ladder at a shutter at the far end of that parade at about a quarter to nine **on his own**, folding the ladder and carrying it twenty yard to the next one · a van at the fish shop at ten past nine with no lettering on either side, standing about ten minutes and not coming back · **a man toward the foot of that street carrying something at about half past nine and back down it at about a quarter to ten without it, and nine indoors, and not one of the nine going to the window** · and a shower off the roofs at about ten that does not reach that hall and takes the tar from soft to hard in about four minutes.
- **THE REGISTER IS AT NINETEEN, THE LINE UNDER IT IS NINE HUNDRED AND FIFTY-FIVE DAYS AND IS STILL BLANK, AND THAT IS THE ONE DAY-COUNT IN THE PROSE OF THESE TEN CHAPTERS.** The last of the nineteen is a lamp entered as gone in about November two years ago under a heading kept over for that sort of thing, nothing has been written under that heading since, and the page runs on a hand's width and then gives up about two inches short of the foot of the book, and nobody left that gap. **THE WOMAN OF ABOUT FIFTY-FIVE GIVES THE FIGURE IN HER OWN MOUTH, UNASKED, AND SAYS SHE COUNTED IT HERSELF OFF A CALENDAR AND COUNTED IT TWICE, AND NOBODY IN THAT ROOM ASKS HER HOW LONG SHE HAS BEEN COUNTING AND NOBODY ASKS HER TO STOP.** The man in his forties says nobody's going to check that and she says she is not asking anybody to, and the man in his sixties says it is a lamp.
- **AND FOUR ANSWERS ARE STILL GOING ROUND THAT TABLE.** The number has been four since the twelfth of January, the four under discussion on the eighth of July are the same four, not one of them has been added to or taken off, and nobody has managed to lay two of them alongside each other. **The man in his forties says it will not be by anybody at that table counting, that recasting four as five to see any attempt to render the count as five yields nothing and only increases the labour of everybody at that table, and that this is not a rule and it is not on anything.** The man from the parade says it has been said in that room before, and the man in his forties says it will not be the last time.
- **THE QUESTION IS STILL A QUESTION AND IS A DIFFERENT WORDING AGAIN FROM 0299 AND FROM 0324.** A man in a fleece, **who has not been in that room before in this account**, asks once whether anybody there has ever been asked to do a thing for nothing and turned it down. **It goes nowhere: the man in his forties lays both hands down on that table, says the thing is a question and is going to remain one, and the man in a fleece accepts that, and nobody carried it further.** **There are nine of them and the room will hold nine, eight of them having been in that room in late May, and any one of those eight could be named there in about a minute, and no one ever has.** The man in a fleece hung his coat on the vacant chair, uninvited and unremarked, and the man who does that hall said he would be all right, and nobody asked him anything else.
- **NOBODY IS RESCUED. NOBODY DIES. NOBODY RENEWS ANYTHING. NOTHING WAS MINUTED. NOBODY WAS THANKED AND NOBODY REFUSED TO BE THANKED, AND NEITHER OF THOSE IS STATED. THE ELEVEN STANDING REFUSALS ARE ELEVEN. CARD 0334 IS NOT A CLOSE AND IS NOT CALLED ONE.**

## 11. Still open, and still forbidden to Continuation 0015

Nothing in Volume 04 has been resolved, undone, improved on, restated or answered, and nothing in Batches 0001 to 0005 or in Continuations 0006, 0007, 0008, 0009, 0010, 0011, 0012, 0013 or 0014 may be. Nobody renews the order, nobody expires it, nobody renames the blank term, nobody puts a name in the line on page two, nobody fills the empty square, Roy Albery does not walk again, nobody dies, nobody is rescued by chance, nobody walks again, the eleven refusals are not twelve, the counter in the vestibule answers nothing and was not entered and was not in any scene, the rota is not rebuilt, the second line under Bram Osei's Thursday is not looked at, the register does not move, the letter, the card, the page, the printed sheets, the column, the shop-window card, the crate with a lid and the four bottles all stand, the drawer under the counter in the vestibule is not opened, is not counted, is not asked about and is not mentioned, and the trade-counter compartment is in no chapter of this batch and holds two sheets and neither has been out since the nineteenth of July. **THE QUIET CUT, THE CIVIC ERASURE, THE ORIGIN OF THE COMMUNITY-WATCH TERM, THE THREE-LINE BRASS TOKENS, THE FIRST CHARTER, THE PROXY, THE ROOT CHAMBER, THE LOWGLASS FLOOR, THE BORROWED CITY AND THE NINE HINGES ARE NOT SPENT. FRANCES TOLLEY IS NOT NAMED AND THE READING RULE IS NOT USED. PRUE CALLOWAY'S FINDING IS NOT OPENED AGAIN AND THE CLAIM TO OPEN A DOOR IS SPENT. NO WEIR RESIDENT MAY BE DESCRIBED IN ANYTHING THAT LEAVES A STREET, AND THE FILLING ROUND DOWN COLLIER LANE IS NOT THE WEIR AND THE TWO MAY NEVER BE JOINED. COLIN WHARTON IS NOT NAMED AND NOBODY GOES UP THAT LANE. A WOMAN OF THIRTY-FOUR IS NOT NAMED, REFERENCED, GENDERED OR GIVEN A CONDITION. PARAGRAPH 4.11 IS NOT ANSWERED AND THE COMPLIANCE WEEK IS NOT VERIFIED. NO ROOT TERM EXPIRES AND THE RECLAMATION SEASON IS NOT SPENT, NOT ANTICIPATED AND NOT DESCRIBED AS APPROACHING. THE DRAIN IS NOT TO BE TOUCHED, THE FIGURE OF £1,812 IS NOT TO BE USED, THE ELEVEN CLAIMS ARE NOT TO BE ADDED UP, A THIRTEENTH PAPER IS NOT TO GO IN ANY BAG, NO PERSON IS TO BE DISCOVERED, NOBODY IS A VILLAIN, AND THE RATE HAS NOT MOVED SINCE 1987.**

**AND NOTHING IN THIS BATCH MAY BE RE-SPENT BY THE NEXT ONE, AND THE FULL LIST OF IT IS AT §16 OF THE CONTINUATION 0014 BLOCK IN `state/continuity.md`. SPECIFICALLY: THE WHITE VAN ACROSS THE FIRST GATE AND THE WHITE VAN ACROSS THE SECOND GATE AND THE TWO PEOPLE WHO WERE NOT TOLD ABOUT EACH OTHER; THE FIRM NINE MILES OFF AND THE QUESTION THAT WAS NOT ANSWERED AND THE BAR THAT IS OFF A BACK DOOR; THE SASH AND THE DOOR ON ONE LANDING AND THE MAN WHO CAME OUT TWICE IN HIS SOCKS; THE TWO WRONG VERSIONS OF A COLLECTION DAY AND THE SIXTY NOTICES NOBODY HAS READ; THE GLAZED LANTERN AND THE BOWL AND THE BIN ON TWO STUDS; THE THREE GATES IN WRAPPERS AND THE NUMBER THREE SAID OUT LOUD AND THE BARROW AT SIX IN THE MORNING; THE THREE LENGTHS AGAINST A BRICK WALL AND THE ADMISSION AND THE QUARTER OF AN INCH RUBBED OUT; THE CARPET AND THE CHEST AND THE FOUR SECONDS; ONE DAY IN WHICH THAT LAD WAS ASKED NOTHING AND SAID SOMETHING TO THREE PEOPLE IN A ROOM; AND THE EIGHTH OF JULY. A FIFTH COST IS NOT A REPEAT AND MAY NOT BE INVENTED. A FIFTH THING A BUILDING DID MAY NOT BE INVENTED EITHER, AND THE LANTERN IS NOW SPENT. A SLATE ON A GABLE IS NOT A STEP IN A ROAD. A THING IN A YARD IS NOT A REPEAT IF IT IS THE SAME SORT OF THING, AND THREE GATES IN WRAPPERS IN ONE LANE IS SPENT AS A WHOLE. **AND ONE DEVICE IS NOW SPENT AS A WHOLE A SEVENTH TIME, AND THIS IS THE FLAG AND NOT A FINDING: THE LAD OF ABOUT EIGHTEEN GOING THROUGH FIVE ROOMS IN WHICH NOBODY PUT ONE TASK TO HIM HAS NOW BEEN DONE IN 0283, 0293, 0303, 0313, 0323 AND 0333, WHICH IS SIX TIMES, AND IN 0313 IT WAS SIX DAYS, IN 0323 IT WAS ONE DAY WITH FOUR EMPTY ROOMS, AND IN 0333 IT IS ONE DAY WITH FOUR OF THE FIVE ROOMS FULL AND THE THING HE SAYS SAID ALOUD IN FRONT OF OTHER PEOPLE RATHER THAN TO A BACK. THE DEVICE IS DONE IN 0311, 0321 AND 0331, WHICH IS THREE TIMES ON THAT ENGINE, AND 0331 WAS ITSELF REWRITTEN SO THAT HE ADMITS SAYING IT INSTEAD OF DENYING IT, SO THE FOURTH TIME ON THE PAGE IS THE THIRD TIME ON THE ENGINE. THE SUBJECT AND THE DEVICE ARE NOT THE SAME THING AND A CARD MAY KEEP THE SUBJECT: A MAN WHO SAID AT A DOOR THAT HE WOULD DO A THING MAY STILL HAVE IT. WHAT IS SPENT IS A MAN SAYING IT OUT LOAD AND NOT DOING IT. **THE INSTRUCTION AND THE CARD ARE IN DIRECT OPPOSITION, NEITHER IS A WRITER'S TO SETTLE, IT IS CARRIED FORWARD HERE UNRESOLVED A SECOND TIME, A HUMAN MAY RULE EITHER WAY, AND UNTIL A HUMAN RULES THE STANDING INSTRUCTION GOVERNS THE CARD, WHICH MEANS A WRITER WHO CANNOT CHANGE THE ENGINE AND NOT THE FURNITURE DOES NOT WRITE 0339.****
