# VOLUME 05 OPEN THREADS — after Continuation 0015, Chapter 344, Wednesday 16 August 2017

**Read with the "VOLUME 05, CONTINUATION 0015 — CHAPTERS 335–344" block at the end of `state/continuity.md`, which carries the canon for these ten chapters and governs wherever the two differ, and with the "VOLUME 05, CONTINUATION 0014 — CHAPTERS 325–334" block above it, which still governs everything those ten chapters did. **VOLUME 05 IS NOT CLOSED AND IS NOT DECLARED CLOSED. THE SERIES IS AT 344 OF 720. NOTHING IN CONTINUATION 0015 RESOLVED ANYTHING AND NOTHING IN IT MAY BE UNDONE, IMPROVED ON, RESTATED OR ANSWERED. EVERY DAY-COUNT BELOW IS CALCULATED FROM ITS ANCHOR ON THE DAY IN QUESTION AND NOT CARRIED FORWARD FROM A SUMMARY: at Wednesday 16 August 2017 the blank line under the nineteenth is nine hundred and ninety-four days, the card in the slot is nine hundred and eight, and the letter in the tray is nine hundred and ten.**

## 1. A warm wall, and a bollard reburied

- **MAJOR TURN: THE BUILDING DOES A SIXTH THING, SHE IS STILL IN THAT ROOM ON THE SAME TERMS, SOMEBODY ELSE FIXES SOMETHING ELSE, AND THE TWO FIXES ARE NOT CONNECTED BY ANYBODY.** The sixth thing is heat and it is not water: a wall on the back side of the room she has never left has been warm to the touch since about the first of July, in a patch as wide as she is and running higher up than she can reach, present through the daylight and gone by four in the morning, and it is shared with a house that has a kitchen at the back of it.
- **She has shoved the bed two foot along that wall, which carried the bulb on its flex over the edge of the mattress; she has the window open from about eight until about nine with a plastic chair under it for her feet; and on each of three days over twenty-eight degrees she has stood a bowl of cold water on that floor beside the bed.** The damp strip at the end wall has not got bigger and the mark on that ceiling is unchanged. Nobody has set foot on that roof since mid-June and nobody means to go up there in July, and nobody has asked her a question about any of it.
- The woman in the room next door along came out once, put it to her that there was something behind that wall, was told she had it right, and set it on the kitchen at number thirty-one. **Two words came back and she gave nothing further, and her own door went back to where it always stands.**
- **Three streets off, on the Thursday of the same week, the man in his forties lifted a bollard out of a road** the day after a shopper with a bag caught her ankle on it, went a foot deeper than the hole that came out, bedded it on rubble and dry fixings, brought it true against the kerb, filled and trowelled, in about an hour, and none of the four houses within thirty yards has been told. A man who is on nothing watched for four minutes, asked whether the work was for somebody, was told no, and went on. **The two are four hundred yard apart with a parade between them and no living person knows about both.**
- **THE WOMAN OF ABOUT FIFTY-FIVE IS NOT HOUSED BY ANYBODY IN THAT CHAPTER. NOBODY TOLD HER WHO PUT THE HASP ON, WHO CAPPED THE OUTLET, WHO PUT THE STOP TAP BACK ON ACROSS, OR WHO WAS ON THAT ROOF, AND NOBODY TOLD HER ABOUT THE BOWL, BECAUSE NOBODY KNOWS.**

## 2. A wireless set, taken up and put down somewhere else

- **MAJOR TURN: THE THING IS PUT DOWN, IT IS TAKEN UP AGAIN, IT IS PUT DOWN SOMEWHERE ELSE, AND THE ONE PERSON WHO KNOWS BOTH PLACES SAW ONE OF THEM AND WAS NOT IN THE OTHER, AND NOBODY PUTS THOSE TWO TOGETHER.** The object is a valve-era wireless in a brown moulded case about the size of a serving tray, with cloth over the speaker, a clean dial window and a crack along one top corner under tape gone the colour of weak tea, and it works off a battery that has been inside it longer than anybody on that parade has been trading. It stood against a store wall in a yard about eleven foot across behind the third of nine shops.
- It was there through the last week of May, on the Monday after, on the last Friday in June, and on the Wednesday of the fifth of July when the man in his thirties was in that yard for six minutes over a cold-feed bracket that needed nothing. He left it and thought about it for the length of four seconds.
- **He came back the following Thursday with his bag, told nobody, laid it on his boards under a folded blanket, and carried it roughly two mile on foot, on the ground that a man with one of these sitting still in a van looks like a man who has stopped for something and will be asked why.** He slowed outside the shop where the woman works, stood on the step about a minute, went in, and said four sentences, **three of which concerned the damage to the case and none of which concerned where the set had stood.**
- She put it on a high shelf in the back room on a folded newspaper and was explicit about four things: not for sale, not in the window, not out for repair, and if a person comes for it they can have it. She said she would not be the woman in this parade holding somebody's wireless. **She did not ask where it came from and he did not say, and neither has mentioned it since.**
- **SHE IS THE ONLY PERSON WHO KNOWS BOTH PLACES: nine visits to that yard in seven weeks, mostly without a reason, one proper look at the set on the last Friday in June, not one word to a living soul, and no presence in that yard on the morning it came out.** The aerial is not on it and the old set inside it is for a bin at the back of that shop. **A THING THAT IS MOVED IS NOT A THING THAT HAS BEEN LOOKED AFTER, A PERSON WHO PICKS A THING UP IS NOT A PERSON WHO HAS ADOPTED IT, AND WHAT HE WOULD HAVE SAID ABOUT ITS HISTORY IF ANYBODY HAD PUT IT TO HIM IS NOTHING, AND HE WOULD NOT HAVE SAID IT IN ANY CASE, BECAUSE THERE IS NO ANSWER HE COULD GIVE THAT WOULD HOLD UP AFTERWARDS.**

## 3. A clock off its bracket, two questions, and one of them unanswerable

- **MAJOR TURN: THE SENTENCE GETS TWO PLACES FURTHER AND THE ROOM IT REACHES ASKS TWO QUESTIONS, AND NEITHER IS ANSWERED, AND ONE OF THE TWO COULD NOT BE ANSWERED IF SOMEBODY WANTED TO.** The man in his sixties said in the hall on a meter that a clock has been lifted off the bracket it stood on above a shop that closed in March, that he compared both clocks on that stretch and identified which was missing, that he saw nobody at the work, that he was not there on the Friday morning, that the man now trading there is not the man the clock was fitted for, that the fitting dates from about the twenties, and that the present occupier was never asked what was over his door.
- The man in his forties recorded nothing. **He restated it in a shop in six sentences of his own, and when the woman would not let it join the other reports he gave the whole of his reasoning: that room has been handed four things and not two of them can be set down beside one another, he cannot say in an afternoon what the four already standing there come to, he has done nothing about any of them and would do nothing about this one even if it stood by itself, AND THAT IS NOT A RULE, BECAUSE THERE IS NOTHING HERE FOR A RULE TO ATTACH TO.** Somebody counted once, got four, and none of the four has shifted since, and that is the mechanism by which four stays four.
- **The man who works nights put two questions and neither was answered.** One concerned which of the two clocks had gone; nobody was asked a second time. **The other concerned whether it had been right while it hung there, and that one could not be answered by anybody who wanted it answered: a clock lifted off a bracket by no one, on a day when no one was working, leaves no record of its own accuracy; the man who regulated it died thirty years back; the man who supplied it died before that; the bracket is part of a building that has never been anybody's responsibility; and the only man who might have watched it lifted was driving a lorry elsewhere.** The man in his forties was at the front of that same building buying screws and heard none of it.
- **A HALL ON A METER IS NOT A COUNCIL. NO FIFTH OF ANYTHING WAS PRODUCED, THE THIRD ANSWER WAS NOT CORRECTED AND NOT ADDED TO, THE FOURTH WAS NOT CALLED A FIFTH, AND NOBODY WAS TOLD WHICH DAY ANY COLLECTION COMES ON. NOBODY WENT TO LOOK AT ANYTHING AND NOBODY FOUND A DOCUMENT.**

## 4. Two small things on one day, and the line not drawn

- **MAJOR TURN: TWO SMALL THINGS HAPPEN, ONE PERSON COULD PUT THEM TOGETHER, THE CHAPTER DOES NOT, AND THE ONE PERSON WHO COULD IS IN NEITHER PLACE.** A fish shop on that parade was found closed at about a quarter past nine on a Friday morning, bar across and padlock on and no light inside. **The man in a fleece passed it on his way to work and did not stop; two women at the bus stop disagreed about whether the closure meant anything and never spoke to each other again; by ten to twelve the premises were trading and the man behind the counter greeted a daily customer as though nothing had happened; the man who puts shutters up carried his ladder past the open door and went on to the next one. Nobody asked the fishmonger anything, then or since, and he has not offered anything.**
- At about one, in a yard off the second house on the left as you go up a street off the top of Marsh Lane, the man in his thirties was four minutes short of a slow cold run because **that side gate has been rehung so that it swings the wrong way and shuts hard against the brick, with a packing of card and a bright screw in the top hanger and dug-and-refilled ground at the hinge post that has not settled.** A woman at the fourth house on that side has been parking a car in that yard through the evenings since the first week of July; he has not approached her and does not intend to. He attended to his own work, found the gate needed pulling to get out, told nobody and has not been up that street since.
- **THE ONLY PERSON GIVEN BOTH ACCOUNTS IS THE WOMAN WHO WORKS IN A SHOP, AND SHE IS IN NEITHER PLACE.** She was told the first at about ten by a man she has not spoken to since March and the second at about half past four by a man who picked his bag up off her step. **She offered no connection and he offered none, and it is not a thing anybody has tried, because a shopkeeper shutting for a morning is a thing that happens to a shopkeeper and a gate hung on the wrong side is a thing that happens to a gate; what the two reports share between them is a person who heard both.** TWO THINGS HAPPENING ON ONE DAY IS NOT A PATTERN AND MAY NOT BE DESCRIBED AS ONE.

## 5. Three letters out of a sign, and a bucket under the holes

- **MAJOR TURN: HE SAYS IT AGAIN AND DOES NOT DO IT, THE PERSON WHO REPEATS IT HAS HEARD IT FROM TWO MEN AND NOT FROM HIM, AND ALL THREE OF THEM ARE BEING STRAIGHT.** A shop that closes at about half past three carries its name in individually cut letters along fourteen foot of pressed aluminium, **and in the second week of June three of those letters released their fixings. Two were recovered, one on a flat roof and one in a gutter, and the third has never been found. The vacated holes are pencil width, the seating rings are still round them, the paint is burnt back in three small circles, rain comes in and reaches the counter a little way back from the front of it, AND A PLASTIC BUCKET HAS STOOD THERE UNDER THE MIDDLE OF THE THREE SINCE THAT SAME WEEK, REFILLED INTO THE SINK SEVERAL TIMES A DAY AND KEPT OFF THE FLOOR, BECAUSE A BUCKET LEFT ON THAT FLOOR GETS WALKED OVER.**
- In that week, on that doorstep, the man in his thirties undertook to have the three letters renewed and refitted before the month was out. Four living people heard him. It is the twenty-fourth of July, the letters are out and the bucket is on the counter.
- **On the Monday morning he repeated the undertaking before anyone had asked him a question, and he heard the sentence go out of him and stay there.** The woman who runs that shop told him the bucket had been on that counter since the week of his first promise, and he replied that that was the third time and that she was at liberty to keep count, and about two hours later he gave the same undertaking to a woman with a clipboard and called it the fourth. The pad of work notes was in his bag and it stayed shut in there the whole time he stood in that road.
- **The woman with a clipboard halted under that sign, because three letters missing out of a run of fourteen is the class of thing her sheet is for, and he gave her the same undertaking before she had opened her mouth, and told her that that was the fourth time he had said it out loud, which is the fourth on the page and the third on that engine.** Her version came from two other men and not from him — a man in a fleece at the shelter at the top end some three weeks back, and the man who does that hall on Carrow Lane, who put the same to a man in his forties back in June, and neither gave a name. **What has spread through the borough is that a sign is going to be done; what he has now said four times is that three letters are going to be made; the two claims are not the same claim, one of them is worth a good deal more to that shop than the other, and there is no means in this borough of deciding which of the two listeners is right.** She declined to enter it, observed that about two lines of space remained on the sheet and meant to spend them on a stand and a grating, and said that a man in a road promising something is a thing that happened in a road.
- **THE THING IS NOT A STEP, A SLATE, A LENGTH OF CLOSE-BOARD, A GUTTER, A DOWNPIPE OR A FENCE POST. A LIST IS NOT A DOCUMENT, A LIST IS NOT A FORM, THERE IS NO FORM IN THIS BOROUGH, A THING SAID OUT LOUD IS NOT A THING ANYBODY KEEPS, AND NOBODY WAS GIVEN A TITLE, A GRADE, A DEPARTMENT, A CHAIR, A SEAT, A MEMBERSHIP, A ROW OR A WAY OF BEING THANKED.**

## 6. A refusal, then an acceptance, on one doorstep in one month

- **MAJOR TURN: THE REFUSAL IS FIRST AND THE ACCEPTANCE IS SECOND, ON THE SAME DOORSTEP, IN THE SAME MONTH, AND THE PERSON WHO BROUGHT BOTH NEVER HEARD ABOUT EITHER AND WILL NOT ASK.** **IT IS NOT A CARPET, IT IS NOT A CHEST, IT IS NOT A VACUUM CLEANER, IT IS NOT A SAUCEPAN, AND NOTHING IN IT CAME OUT OF A SKIP OR A YARD.**
- **The sixth of July: a steel bed base, folded once, with a foam mattress rolled and strapped against it, the frame painted on one face only, the webbing tight enough to have marked the foam, standing across the step from the door frame to the handrail.** She came to the door with her coat on, looked at what was on the step rather than at him, and said no. **He did not ask for a reason and produced no words of any kind, and the four or five seconds in which she said it and he took hold of the straps again carried nothing whatever out of either of them.** It went back down in three loads, left with the remainder of that load in the third week of the month, and cannot be followed from here.
- **The thirtieth: a small tiled folding table, about three foot by two foot, one hinge stiff, with a ring on the frame where somebody once set a cup down.** He got it up in one carry, knocked twice, described it, said she could turn him away, and was told one word before he had finished turning. She used it once: she had it standing beside that bed for the better part of a morning while she took the blanket off the foot of the bed — the one folded in three there since the winter and kept there against a draught off that landing door — threw it across the table and worked it with a brush kept on the sill, and about nine days of grey came out of the middle of it, and with the sash open the dust lifted off in one slow wave, and twice she had to stop and get at her ears. She folded the blanket again in three, put it back across the bed, and stood the table on its end against that wall under the window, clear of the carpet that stands on its side along the same wall by the width of a foot. **Nothing is on it and it is not a place for anything.**
- **At about half past four on the thirtieth she had him at her counter for four minutes. He would not name the two items and gave the reason: a description would put her outside that door with something in front of her to be courteous about, and neither of them wanted that.** A GIFT THAT IS REFUSED IS NOT A FALLING-OUT, A GIFT THAT IS TAKEN IS NOT A PURCHASE, AND A PERSON WHO REFUSES DOES NOT OWE A REASON AND IS NOT OWED A QUESTION.

## 7. A torch that is his own, and nobody asking him about it

- **MAJOR TURN: HE HAS THE THING ALL MORNING AND NOBODY ASKS HIM ABOUT IT, HE TELLS ONE PERSON ABOUT IT, AND THAT PERSON DOES NOTHING WITH WHAT HE SAYS.** **THE CHAPTER IS TWO PLACES AND ONE MORNING. IT IS NOT FIVE ROOMS, IT IS NOT A DAY, AND THE ONE THING HE SAYS IS ANSWERED AND THEN NOTHING IS DONE WITH THE ANSWER, WHICH IS THE ONLY DIFFERENCE LEFT TO MAKE AFTER SIX RUNS OF A THING SAID THAT NOBODY ANSWERS.**
- **The object is a rubber-bodied hand torch, about the length of a forearm, bought in June from a man with a trestle table of old electrical goods at a market four miles off for two pounds, with three letters and a stroke cut into the underside of the case in a nail inside a rectangle where a different name had been, which are not his initials and are not going to come off.** It lives on a shelf by the kitchen door, stood on end on a folded tea towel, and its bulb has been a yellow one rather than a white one since the middle of July. He is about eighteen, washes up four mornings a week, and **nobody in that kitchen has ever put a question to him about where it came from.**
- It went through four pairs of hands in one morning and nobody asked him anything: the man who cooks there used it and handed it back warm, a woman who eats there twice a week carried it through to the cold room and returned it with the switch on, the man at the table remarked on its provenance and price and the woman beside him said the price was the price **and neither of them was addressing the lad, who was at the sink and heard all of it**, and then it sat up on that fridge with its switch on for the best part of four hours. **At ten past twelve the man who cooks in it told him to take it out of the kitchen and said that if it is still on that fridge in a fortnight it goes in the bin and he will not be the one saying so twice.**
- **He carried it and a bucket up to a first-floor corridor in a building by the water, where it throws about nine foot of yellow and the bulb is on its way out, and he went the length of the mop as he goes it every time, over a floor that has needed nothing since before Christmas, because he has never learned how anyone else would come to know that.** The woman of about fifty-five came out with a jug in each hand. She called it a lamp. He corrected her on the three counts that mattered: that it is a torch, that he paid two pounds for it at a market in June, and that his own name is cut under the case. **She asked one practical question, which was whether it works. He said it lights about nine foot and the bulb is going and will not be white again. She said right, went past with both jugs, said nothing about the market or the trader or what a torch has to do with anything, did not ask him where he had got it, and did nothing whatever with anything he had said.**
- **The woman of about forty-two came through that kitchen on her way to her own hall with a bag of cups about ten past two, said the light in the hall had gone and that she has no bulb and is not going up a ladder, and he handed the torch over without a word; it came back in the early afternoon, having done ten foot of light in that hall, and she said what that hall wanted was to own a bulb rather than depend on one. THE BULB IN THAT HALL IS STILL UNBOUGHT.** The three key-holding women were not put in one sentence in that chapter. **NO THANKS OF ANY KIND APPEARS IN THAT CHAPTER AND NONE APPEARS ANYWHERE IN THIS BATCH.**

## 8. Five feet of railing, and one lane name on a blank page

- **MAJOR TURN: THE BUILDING DOES AN ORDINARY THING TO NOBODY, SOMEBODY GOES THROUGH A LIST OF NOTHING AND FINDS ONE THING ON IT, THE ONE THING IS NOT WHAT THE LIST IS FOR, AND NEITHER FACT CHANGES ANYTHING.** Five feet of wall-mounted iron railing on one side of a passage between the back of a shop and the gable of a house has drawn a fixing right out and pulled off the brick, and hangs from the remaining bolt with its bars down, its lowest part about five feet above the paving. **A traffic cone has been set on the footway beneath it since Saturday afternoon with a scrap of cardboard under the base, and a man on that parade would put money on having set it there himself, has said nothing about it and has not been asked.** The footway is five foot wide, about thirty people pass through a day in August, every one of them has looked up, and not one has mentioned it to anybody else in the passage.
- **The man in his forties passed under it at about eight, about ten past nine and about one, and has used that passage on his way to that parade since the last week of March.** He looked up the first two times, looked up the third and said nothing to himself, and has not looked up since. He could have asked in the shop at the front; the woman there does not know the wall is on her side and would say it is not hers, and then ownership would have to be pursued, and no answer obtainable on that route is better than walking under it. **He did not report it and the woman who works in a shop, looking down that passage from her own step at about ten, did not urge him to.** She has two previous dealings with that street, one about a hedge and one about a street light, and both letters asked the same question, which is why has it not been done, and what came back in both cases was a slip carrying a date and a figure and nothing else — the only answer she has ever had to anything she has sent anywhere — and nobody has told her what it signifies. He offered that one body counted a hedge and a different body wrote the number down and neither is going to tell her which hedge.
- **At about half past four he took his pad out of its box in the van to look for a length he had written down somewhere, and on a sheet he opened in June, wrote on once and never touched again, there was a single item: the name of a lane, in pencil, on the second ruled line, with nothing after it and nothing above it.** He knows that lane; it is reached from a road about a mile off, comes to an end at a yard, and he has been down it twice, once in March for a man who wanted a roof off a garage and then stopped wanting it, and once in May because it shortens a route he then undid, and there is a man at the far end with pallets stacked up, a van and a dog that barks at passing traffic, and he has never gone into that yard and never spoken to that man. **He cannot say what work the note refers to, or whether it referred to work at all, or in which of those places he wrote it, and there is nothing available to find out with, because a sheet of a pad riding about in a van carries no date, and the next time he writes there the lane name will lie under about nine further lines in about four months with nothing to separate it from any of them.** A LIST OF NOTHING IS NOT A FORM AND A LIST OF NOTHING IS NOT A DOCUMENT, NOBODY WAS ADDED TO THAT PAGE AND NOBODY WAS TAKEN OFF IT, AND **A LANE NAME ON A PAGE IN A VAN IS NOT A MUTUALLY AGREED RIGHT OF RETURN AND IS NOT A LANE ANYBODY LIVES IN AND IS IN NO DOCUMENT AND NAMES NO PERSON.**

## 9. The same six words, twice, and two readings of them

- **MAJOR TURN: THE SAME SENTENCE GOES OUT TWICE AND COMES BACK TWICE AS TWO DIFFERENT THINGS, AND THE PERSON WHO SAID IT CANNOT TELL WHICH HE MEANT, AND HE DOES NOT FIND OUT.** Six corrugated sheets, each about four foot by two, rusted on the underside of the top one, are propped against the side wall of the last house on a street off the Cleeve road, having gone up there in the second week of July when the roof came off an outbuilding at the foot of that street, with grass grown about a foot up the bottom two. **The footway is six foot wide, the sheets take two foot of it and four foot remains, and nothing has ever been said about them in public except that they are a hazard to anybody carrying a bag.** The man who put them there lives at the end of the street, has done for nine years, and is on nothing.
- Said once, in the middle of the morning, to the man in his forties as he passed the step, in six words, being an objection to a proposal that was never actually made; the man in his forties went ten foot, came back, asked the question a person asks when told not to do something that was not proposed, was told what not to do, and was given no reason and asked for none. **He kept off that side of the footway on each of his four visits that day. A man halted in a street here either does as he is asked or says straight out that he will not, there is nothing else available to him, and he used the second form and said it in nine seconds.**
- Said again, at about a quarter past one, in the shop where the woman works, to her, with a length of batten in his hand he had not come in for, **and what she took from it, and has kept, is that a man was telling her to keep it out of her window, and that reading is not incorrect and is not what he meant.** He said he was not going to say what to, that this was the whole of it, that he had already used the same six words on the man in the road that morning and that man was as entitled to them as she was, and that he would not tell her which of the two he had said more to. She told him he had said it to the other man first, and he told her that man may do as he likes with it. He paid for the batten.
- **A woman with a bag went into the road twice that day, once around the middle of the day and once in the early afternoon, said nothing either time, and was watched both times from the step. At about half past four the man in his forties came past in the opposite direction and a man in a fleece asked whether anything was being done about the sheets, and was told that nothing had been asked of him about them, and that was the whole of what the questioner wanted. NOBODY WAS TOLD WHAT THE MAN MEANT AND NOBODY IS GOING TO BE, AND NO FURTHER MENTION OF IT EXISTS IN THIS BOROUGH.**

## 10. One ordinary Wednesday in August, in the order of the clock

- **THE BOROUGH IS ORDINARY AND THE MONTH IS A MONTH AND NOT A TURN OF ANYTHING, AND CARD 0344 IS NOT A CLOSE AND IS NOT CALLED ONE.** warm at ten to six, cloud off the water at about half past nine with no rain in it, the kind of day afterwards described as one on which nothing happened · **a van driven up that parade in the small hours and left with two wheels on the footway, and the man who puts shutters up arriving with his ladder a quarter of an hour after that and finding he could not get it up against that shop, so working the other two and coming back in the middle of the morning to do that one from the foot of the ladder, while the van went at about ten past nine and nobody ever got out of it** · a man with a sack over his shoulder standing about two minutes outside a shop that closes early and then going on his way with the sack still on his shoulder · a key to a flat over three shops, an outside stair at about half past seven and down at about twenty to eight, and a man in a fleece at the top-of-the-parade stop on the other side of the road at the same time, neither looking over · **four lengths of treated batten set down in the middle of a yard some nine foot from the place that yard stacks its timber by a man with a barrow who left without saying where he was going, and carried across one at a time by the wrong man** · two empty jugs down that outside stair and up it again, one hooked over the other, with a kettle over her other arm, which is the errand she has been up and down that stair twice a day all summer, and nobody offering her anything and her asking nobody for anything · a bus that came, stopped, went on, was not his, and half an hour of waiting after it · a woman with a clipboard at the end of what she had with about two lines of room left, who spent one on a stand and kept the other · a man in a doorway eating out of a paper bag and a stranger wishing him good afternoon · a woman in the Carrow Lane hall for an hour with a newspaper, who asked the man in his sixties whether it was open, was told it was, and neither asked the other for anything.
- **THE FOUR, IN A DAYTIME HALL WITH TWO MEN IN IT: the man in his sixties asked how many of those things there are now; the man in his forties said four without interrupting what he was doing with a cloth; the man in his sixties said he would have put it at five; and the man in his forties said the number is four, has been four since the twelfth of January, that he has entered nothing and removed nothing, and that the man in his sixties has not been in this borough long enough to have been here in January and neither has anybody who was not in that room in January. The man in his sixties took that as fair enough and went for more tea. The remaining three went unremarked by both of them and neither of them could have put names to them. The count has not moved, nothing has been added to it, nothing has been taken off it, and no two of the four can be set beside one another.**
- **THE QUESTION, ON A CORNER, AT ABOUT A QUARTER PAST FOUR, ASKED ONCE: that nothing in this town has ever been counted by anybody, and whether anybody had ever explained to the man in his forties why the number is four. He said nobody had. The man in a fleece said right and went on down the parade. NOTHING WAS ANSWERED.**
- **THE REGISTER IS AT NINETEEN, THE LINE UNDER IT IS NINE HUNDRED AND NINETY-FOUR DAYS AND IS STILL BLANK, AND THAT IS THE ONE DAY-COUNT IN THE PROSE OF THESE TEN CHAPTERS, ON THIS CHAPTER'S OWN DATE, IN THE BODY, AND IT IS PRINTED ONCE.** The nineteenth is a yard lamp, entered as gone about two years back in November, under a heading reserved for that class of thing, and nothing has been entered under that reserved heading since; past it the page goes on a couple of fingers and then stops short of the lower edge, and nobody closed the gap. **He went in alone on the afternoon of the sixteenth, climbed to that bay, opened the door that has never latched properly, held the page flat under a thumb, did the count in his head, wrote nothing at all, and came back down again without shutting that door behind him as it should be shut.**
- **NOBODY IS RESCUED. NOBODY DIES. NOBODY RENEWS ANYTHING. NOBODY IS THANKED AND NOBODY REFUSES TO BE THANKED, AND NEITHER OF THOSE IS STATED. THE ELEVEN STANDING REFUSALS ARE ELEVEN.**

**AND THE VERBATIM-SPAN CHECK, WHICH WAS RUN AT SIX RUNS AND EVERY WINDOW AND EVERY THRESHOLD NAMED, THE SCRIPT COMPARING TOKENS AND NOT CHARACTERS, **AND WHOSE FIRST TAKING FOUND SEVENTY-THREE REUSES IN THE SIX STATE FILES THIS BATCH HAD JUST WRITTEN AND FIVE MORE ACROSS THREE NAMED CHAPTER WINDOWS, EVERY ONE OF THEM REAL, AND EVERY ONE REPAIRED. **THE FIGURE THAT BINDS IS THE FINAL ONE AND IT IS NIL ON EVERY WINDOW, AND THE FIGURES THAT WENT WITH IT ARE RUN ONE 0 RAW AND NIL, RUN TWO 11 RAW AND NIL, RUN THREE 23 / 17 / 11 RAW AND NIL IN ALL THREE NAMED WINDOWS, RUN FOUR 46 RAW AND NIL, RUN FIVE 46 RAW AND NIL, AND SIX IS THE COUNT OF RUNS AND NOT A RUN, WITH EVERY RAW SPAN COUNTED IN ONE OF THE THREE EXCLUDED CLASSES AND NOT CHASED. **THE 325–334 WINDOW IS THE ONE THE CHAPTERS IMMEDIATELY ABOVE ARE IN AND IT MUST BE NAMED IN EVERY PROMPT AFTER THIS ONE WITH ITS FIGURE, WHICH IS 23 RAW AND NIL. **THE METHOD IS AT §12A OF THE CONTINUATION 0015 BLOCK AND ITS FIVE PARTS THAT ARE EASY TO GET WRONG ARE: LOWERCASE FIRST AND THEN `[a-z0-9']+`; A TOKEN THAT IS NOTHING BUT APOSTROPHE MARKS IS DROPPED; **A PARAGRAPH BOUNDARY IS A SENTINEL NO TOKEN CAN EQUAL, ON BOTH SIDES, IN THE TARGET AND IN EVERY SET FILE, BECAUSE A WALK THAT EXTENDS AN N-GRAM HIT WITH THE NEXT N-GRAM HIT CAN STEP OVER A GAP AND REPORT A RUN THAT IS NOT ONE STRING IN EITHER FILE**; EVERY SPAN IS EXTENDED BY DIRECT COMPARISON FROM A REAL POSITION AND NOT BY ADDING ONE HIT TO THE NEXT; AND EACH FILE OF A COMPARISON SET IS READ SEPARATELY SO THAT A SPAN CANNOT BRIDGE TWO FILES. **AND SIXTH, WHICH THE 0015 PROMPT DID NOT CARRY AND WHICH THE CHECKPOINT PASS BUILT, BECAUSE THE 0014 METHOD RECORDED THE THREE EXCLUSIONS BUT NOT HOW TO DETECT THE FIRST OF THEM: A DATELINE IS DETECTED STRUCTURALLY, AS A PARAGRAPH CARRYING A FOUR-DIGIT YEAR OR A WEEKDAY TOGETHER WITH A MONTH OR A YEAR, AND A SPAN IS ONLY OF THAT CLASS WHEN THE WHOLE OF IT SITS INSIDE SUCH A PARAGRAPH, WHICH IS STRICTER THAN A CONTAINMENT TEST.**

**AND THE POSITIVE CONTROL IS TWO PAIRS AND A CALIBRATION, AND ALL THREE WERE ASKED FOR BEFORE ANY FIGURE WAS BELIEVED: `state/open-threads.md` AGAINST `state/continuity.md` READ 3,127 RAW SPANS AND A LONGEST RUN OF 165 TOKENS AFTER both files were rewritten in the checkpoint repair, **AND THAT 165 IS LOWER THAN THE 177 THAT THREE EARLIER TAKINGS REPORT, AND THE REASON IS THE ONE THOSE TAKINGS GAVE, WHICH IS THAT A CONTROL IS A CHECK ON THE SCRIPT AND NOT A CONSTANT AND IT MOVES WHENEVER EITHER OF THE TWO FILES IS EDITED, AND BOTH WERE**; 0319 AGAINST 0323 READ 1 RAW SPAN AND 0 REUSES, WHICH REPRODUCES THREE SEPARATE TAKINGS IN THREE EARLIER BLOCKS AND IS THE ONE CONTROL NO REPAIR TOUCHED; AND A FOURTEEN-TOKEN PLANT FROM 0319 RETURNED EXACTLY ONE SPAN OF FOURTEEN. **A LATER WRITER WHO FINDS 3,127, OR 2,706, OR 803, OR 742, OR 752, OR 2,200, AND CANNOT REPRODUCE IT MUST TREAT IT AS UNMEASURED AND RE-TAKE IT.**

**AND THE SEVENTY-EIGHT REUSES ARE NOT THE ONLY THINGS THE PASSES ON THIS BATCH FOUND, AND THE FIGURE 23 THAT THE PREVIOUS PRINTING OF THIS BLOCK GAVE FOR RUN FOUR IS WITHDRAWN AND WAS NOT A COUNT OF ANYTHING. THE FIGURES AT ZERO THAT WERE NOT ZERO ON THE FIRST TAKING ARE ELEVEN (NINE TIMES, SIX OF THEM TIMES OF DAY), *SPRING* (FIVE TIMES AS A SEASON), *FORTY* FOR A COUNT OF TOUCHES IN A DAY, *DRAIN-* IN A YARD, *DRAWER* IN A ROOM, *A TUESDAY* AND *A FRIDAY* IN TWO BODIES, AND A *LAMP* ON A BED LIGHT THAT CAME WITHIN ONE WORD OF THE REGISTER'S BULB. **AND ONE THING NO RUN IN THIS REPOSITORY CAN FIND, WHICH IS THE FIGURE CENSUS: THE MAN IN HIS THIRTIES IS IN THREE CHAPTERS AND NOT FOUR, THE MAN IN HIS SIXTIES IN TWO AND NOT THREE, THE MAN WHO DOES THAT HALL IN ONE AND MENTIONED IN TWO, **AND EVERY ONE OF THOSE THREE COUNTS CAME OUT TOO HIGH ON THE FIRST TAKING BECAUSE A SEARCH COUNTS A SENTENCE THAT DENIES A FIGURE WAS THERE, AND THAT IS THE THIRD TIME IN THREE PASSES THAT A CENSUS HAS COUNTED A FIGURE BEING RULED OUT, AND A CENSUS THAT COUNTS A MAN BEING RULED OUT IS A CENSUS OF NAMES AND NOT OF PEOPLE.****



# VOLUME 05 OPEN THREADS — after Continuation 0016, Chapter 354, Sunday 24 September 2017

**Read with the "VOLUME 05, CONTINUATION 0016 — CHAPTERS 345–354" block at the end of `state/continuity.md`, which carries the canon for these ten chapters and governs wherever the two differ, and with the "VOLUME 05, CONTINUATION 0015 — CHAPTERS 335–344" block above it, which still governs everything those ten chapters did. **VOLUME 05 IS NOT CLOSED AND IS NOT DECLARED CLOSED. THE SERIES IS AT 354 OF 720. NOTHING IN CONTINUATION 0016 RESOLVED ANYTHING AND NOTHING IN IT MAY BE UNDONE, IMPROVED ON, RESTATED OR ANSWERED. EVERY DAY-COUNT BELOW IS CALCULATED FROM ITS ANCHOR ON THE DAY IN QUESTION AND NOT CARRIED FORWARD FROM A SUMMARY: at Sunday 24 September 2017 the blank line under the nineteenth is one thousand and thirty-three days, the card in the slot is nine hundred and forty-seven, and the letter in the tray is nine hundred and forty-nine.**

## 1. A smell in a corridor, and a figure nobody was told

- **MAJOR TURN: THE BUILDING DOES A SEVENTH THING, SHE IS STILL IN THAT ROOM ON THE SAME TERMS, SOMEBODY ELSE COUNTS SOMETHING AND GETS A NUMBER THAT IS A NUMBER, AND NEITHER FACT TOUCHES THE OTHER.** The seventh thing is a smell and it is neither water nor heat: it comes into that first-floor corridor and into the room at the end of it in the dark, since about the tenth of the month, and it is gone before the light, and gone by daylight and not there when the wind is off the land, and it is worse at the bottom of the outside stair than at the top of it.
- **WHAT SHE DID, AND NOBODY WAS TOLD ANY OF IT AND NOBODY ASKED HER ANY OF IT:** she left the window open because it was open anyway, checked the one thing in that flat that makes gas and found nothing, and ruled out the pipe somebody matted in the front room years ago by herself, because that one smells in the wrong wind. **ON THE SIXTEENTH SHE COULD NOT SETTLE, AND SHE PUT THE PLASTIC CHAIR SHE KEEPS FOR THE WINDOW OUT ON TO THE LANDING AT ABOUT TWENTY TO FIVE AND SLEPT ON IT, AND SHE WAS UP AND HAD IT BACK UNDER THE WINDOW BY HALF PAST SIX, AND SHE DID IT TWICE.** She is not at anybody's door because anybody arranged it.
- The woman in the room next door along came out once because her own window is on the wrong aspect and she has had it shut since the mornings turned. **She said it was the gas, and that it was not coming out of the room next door because the woman was sat in the corridor, and was told that that was where it was coming from, said right, and put her own door back where she keeps it.** No second exchange and no offer, and no note on any door in that block, and nobody in that block has a telephone, because that block stopped being anybody's job in June.
- **THE MAN IN HIS FORTIES IS THE ONLY REASON THERE ARE TWO ACCOUNTS OF THAT SMELL IN THIS BOROUGH.** He was on that landing for about an hour on somebody else's work and gave her a cause that is real and ordinary — the river coming up under there when the wind is off the water, worse on the stairs than on the landing, a gully at the bottom he has had it off for as long as he can remember — and named a standpipe on that stair nobody has looked at since the year before last and a vent on the back of that block coming out under the second landing, and said the vent was the last thing he would be looking at. She told him the woman next door along had said it was the gas, and he stopped, because he heard a woman in that building get there before he did, and he knew it while he was saying so. **HE HAS NOT BEEN UP THAT STAIR AGAIN, AND BOTH OF THE THINGS THAT WERE SAID ARE TRUE, AND NOBODY IN THAT BUILDING WILL EVER BE TOLD WHICH OF THE TWO WOMEN WAS NEARER IT.**
- **THE COUNT IS A MILE AND A HALF AWAY AND TOUCHES NONE OF IT.** At about half past four that afternoon the woman who works in a shop brought the day's takings up the outside stair at the back of three shops, which she does four times a week, and counted them on her own kitchen table in the only order anybody who has done that for a while would use, and wrote the figure on the back of her own hand and put the money in a safe under her own counter and took the key into her own pocket. It came out about where the other three Thursdays of that month came out. **NOBODY IN THAT SHOP KNOWS WHAT IT CAME TO, NOBODY COULD TELL YOU WHAT THE SHOP TAKES IN A WEEK, SHE HAS NEVER OFFERED IT, AND THERE IS NOBODY IN THIS BOROUGH SHE WOULD TELL.** Neither of those two women has any idea the other one is on the page.
- **THE WOMAN OF ABOUT FIFTY-FIVE IS NOT HOUSED BY ANYBODY IN THAT CHAPTER. NOBODY PUT HER IN THAT ROOM, NOBODY ARRANGED ONE WORD OF HOW SHE LIVES IN IT, NOBODY WAS TOLD WHO PUT THE HASPIED SIDE ON, WHO CAPPED AN OUTLET, WHO PUT THE STOP TAP BACK ON ACROSS, OR WHO WAS ON THAT ROOF, AND NOBODY WAS TOLD ABOUT THE CHAIR OR THE BOWL OR THE COUNT, BECAUSE NOBODY KNOWS ANY OF IT.**

## 2. A ladder, a bench and a back room, and two men who are each certain

- **MAJOR TURN: THE THING IS PUT DOWN, TAKEN UP, PUT DOWN SOMEWHERE ELSE AND TAKEN UP AGAIN, TWO PEOPLE ARE SURE THEY WERE THE LAST TO HAVE IT, NEITHER IS SURE, AND NEITHER WILL ASK THE OTHER.** A four-foot painted wooden step ladder with somebody's spanner on the top tread. **A THING THAT IS MOVED IS NOT A THING THAT HAS BEEN LOOKED AFTER, NOBODY WAS TOLD WHERE IT CAME FROM, NOBODY WAS ASKED, A PERSON WHO PICKS A THING UP IS NOT A PERSON WHO HAS ADOPTED IT, AND THERE IS NO PERSON IN THIS BOROUGH WHO COULD HAVE GRANTED THE USE OF THAT YARD TO ANYBODY AT ALL.**
- Sat 5 Aug, late afternoon: the man in his forties left it in a yard off the Cleeve road with its feet up on a bench, because he did not want to explain to a man in his own workshop why he had taken his own ladder out of his van.
- Sun 6 Aug, morning: the man in his thirties found it, used it for half a morning on somebody's upstairs, put it back where it was, and did not shut a gate he had opened. That afternoon he asked one question about it at a counter in a shop and did not say where it had been. **She said one word and has never asked him a second thing about it.**
- Wed 16 Aug, early afternoon: the man in his forties went into that shop's back room for something else and found the ladder against the far wall with a fortnight of dust round its feet. **He was not the man it had been left with and nobody was in a position to tell him that.** He took the spanner off the top tread into his pocket without deciding to, carried it out past a counter where she was on the telephone, said good afternoon, and put it back on the bench in that yard. He has told nobody he took a thing out of a shop.
- Sun 20 Sep, ten to ten: the man in his thirties went into that shop for a coil of wire, went through to the back room while she was at the rack, and it was under a blanket at the back of his van before a minute had gone. **She saw him go through and come out and charged him for the wire and said the price was dear and he said it was, and neither of them said one word about the ladder.** He has it under a blanket in his van.
- **BOTH ACCOUNTS ARE STRAIGHT AND EACH HAS A HOLE IN IT.** Each man is certain the other never had a hand on it, and the reason each is certain is a fact about his own week and not about the other's. **ASKING IS ONE QUESTION AND THE REPLY TO IT IS THE OTHER QUESTION, AND THERE IS NOT ONE ANSWER BETWEEN THEM THAT WOULD SURVIVE BEING GIVEN.** The spanner is in a pocket in a van and has never been a question to anybody.

## 3. A true sentence, a wrong lane, and a longer walk home

- **MAJOR TURN: THE SENTENCE GETS ONE PLACE FURTHER AND THE ROOM THAT HEARS IT TAKES IT AS A FACT, AND SOMEBODY IN THAT ROOM IS WRONG ABOUT SOMETHING IN IT, AND NOBODY ASKS ANYBODY ANYTHING.** In the hall on a meter the man in his sixties put forward that a house stands empty at the top of a lane, and he gave the address in more detail than anything has been given in that room in months, and **the only person who heard it was a shopkeeper.**
- She gave it to the man who works nights at a counter as a fact rather than as something she had been told, and it came back with two questions, one about which house and one about which road, and she could not answer either. **THE ROAD SHE ATTACHED IT TO WAS NOT THE ROAD IT IS ON.** His lane has a kerb on one side, seven houses in it, a light over the last door and no board on any window in it. The other one is off a different road, a quarter of a mile the other way, and there is a board outside the house in that one.
- She does not know that she was wrong. He did not find out and is not going to, because the only route to finding out runs through a question about a road he cannot name, and a man who asks a woman which road a lane comes off has told her he could not say which road that lane hangs off, and he had somewhere else to be on the Friday of that week whatever she said.
- **WHAT IT HAS COST IS ABOUT TWO HUNDRED YARD OF PAVEMENT AND ABOUT FOUR MINUTES, EVERY NIGHT, FOR FIVE WEEKS.** He has never told anybody he used that lane. There is nothing wrong with the lane and there is a woman in the last house of it with a car on the drive, and the board outside the other house is a different house, and no one here will ever walk up either lane and establish it. The man in his sixties has no way of finding out that he was wrong and would not go and look for himself if he could.
- **A FAMILIAR WRONG ANSWER IS NOT A CONFIRMED ONE AND NOT A WEAKENED ONE AND NOT A SECOND ONE OF THE SAME. THE THIRD WRONG ANSWER WAS NOT CORRECTED AND NOT IMPROVED ON AND NOT ADDED TO, THE FOURTH WAS NOT CALLED A FIFTH, AND NOBODY WAS TOLD WHICH DAY ANY COLLECTION COMES ON.**

## 4. A shutter at half past ten and a man who lost a working day

- **MAJOR TURN: TWO SMALL THINGS HAPPEN, ONE IS ORDINARY AND ONE COSTS SOMEBODY A DAY, ONE PERSON COULD PUT THEM TOGETHER, THE CHAPTER DOES NOT, AND THE ONE PERSON WHO COULD IS NOT IN EITHER PLACE.**
- **THE ORDINARY ONE IS A SHOP OPENING HOUR.** A shopter on that parade went out for her lunch and the man behind the counter put the bar across and hauled the shutter down with about nine people inside and a queue at the card machine, and the rope stuck and then did not, and the shop went dark from the pavement with the light still on inside and the machine visible through the slot. She came back at about one and carried on serving and has never mentioned it to anybody. About thirty people went past in the middle of the day, nine of them looked, and not one mentioned it to the next person. **THE ONLY PERSON TOLD ANYTHING WAS A MAN ON HIS WAY TO A BUS, AND HE TOLD THE WOMAN WHO WORKS IN A SHOP FOUR DAYS LATER IN THAT SHOP, AND SHE AGREED WITH HIM AND SAID THE OBVIOUS THING ABOUT A LUNCH, AND THAT WAS THE WHOLE OF IT.**
- **THE ONE THAT COSTS A DAY IS A TRADESMAN'S MORNING.** He turned up at a house a few minutes before ten for half an hour's work. The woman who lives there told him at the door that she was out until about two, that the buses were hourly, and that it would be as well to come first, and she was sorry about it. He said it was no trouble and did not come at about two. **HE WAITED A LITTLE AND THEN WENT AND SAT IN AN EMPTY CAR PARK FOR THE REST OF THE DAY, BECAUSE THERE WAS WORK IN HIS OWN SHOP PROMISED FOR THE MONDAY AND IT WAS NOT THAT KIND OF WORK AND HE WAS NOT GOING TO STAND IN THAT SHOP ON A FRIDAY AFTERNOON WITH HIS HANDS EMPTY.** He read a paper he had brought with him, which takes about an hour and a half, drank the water he had brought for that, nodded at a man doing a stocktake on a van, watched a woman and a dog at the far kerb, did the job at ten past two in about twenty minutes, and got home at about half past five. About six hours of that day are nobody's, and he has not said one word about that day to anybody.
- **THE ONE PERSON WHO HAS BOTH IS A THIRD OF A MILE FROM BOTH.** She heard the shutter on the Thursday and the day on the Friday, at her own step, with the other man's bag in her hand. **SHE HAS NEVER SPOKEN TO THE MAN WHO TOLD HER ABOUT THE SHUTTER AND COULD NOT TELL YOU HIS NAME, AND THE MAN WHO LOST THE DAY DOES NOT KNOW THAT SHE WAS EVER TOLD ABOUT THE SHUTTER AT ALL.** There is nothing in this borough that puts those two in one breath, and she did not try, and it was not a thing she thought about afterwards. Two things in a week is not a pattern and a coincidence is not a connection.

## 5. A fifth time, and somebody else's hands on a ladder

- **MAJOR TURN: HE SAYS IT AGAIN AND DOES NOT DO IT, SOMEBODY ELSE HEARD IT FROM TWO MEN AND NOT FROM HIM, ALL THREE OF THEM ARE BEING STRAIGHT, AND THE CONSEQUENCE OF THE SENTENCE IS SOMEBODY ELSE'S HANDS AND NOT A WORD.**
- **THE THING IS A BIRD BOX THE SIZE OF A SHOEBOX** on the gable end of a house at the bottom of a street off the Cleeve road, painted the colour of the render, with a chip out of the front lip, and nobody on that street knows who fixed it there. **THERE IS OTHER WORK ON THAT SAME GABLE WHICH IS NOT THE BIRD BOX:** four foot of soft mortar joints, priced in June, chased twice, and refused in July because three other people were being asked about it. The box has been on that wall the whole time and neither man in this story has ever referred to it.
- **HE HAS SAID HE WILL HAVE A BOX MADE AND SCREWED UP THERE BEFORE THE END OF SEPTEMBER FIVE TIMES, TO FOUR PEOPLE, AND THE FIFTH WAS THIS MORNING to a man who wanted nothing from him.** The other four went to a woman at her own gate in July, to the man in his forties in a yard over a length of batten, to a man in a fleece on a parade, and to the woman who works in a shop at her counter — **and on that last occasion his work notes were out of the bag and in his hand the band round it off, and she watched him slip it back into the bag with nothing written on it, and she told him he was not going to write that down, and he said no, and said he has a line in there for three letters in another sign that he has not opened since the summer before last and is not starting now.**
- **WHAT HAS GONE ROUND THAT STREET IS A DIFFERENT SENTENCE, AND IT CAME FROM TWO MEN WHO HAD NOT HAD IT FROM HIM.** What has gone round is a claim that somebody is going to have that wall seen to. A man in a fleece put that to the man in his forties at a market stall four miles off, and the man in his forties put it to another a man in a fleece a good way above that, and neither gave a name. **THE MAN IN HIS FORTIES HAD HIS OWN QUOTATION ON THAT WALL FROM JUNE AND HAD NOT BEEN GIVEN THE WORK, AND ON TUESDAY AFTERNOON HE PUT THOSE TWO FACTS TOGETHER AND WENT UP THERE WITH A LADDER AND A FLAT BAR.**
- **HE TOOK THE BOX OFF ITS TWO SCREWS AND PUT IT ON THE PATH FACE DOWN AND HE IS NOT GOING TO PUT IT BACK.** The screws came out with two foot of render still on the heads of them. He came back on the Wednesday to do the pointing he had not been given, found the box still on the path, stood and looked at it and left it where it was, **because a man here who restores a fitting to a surface somebody has told him is booked in is a man who has taken the job on.** He did the four foot in about two hours with nobody watching and has not charged anybody and has not mentioned it. The woman who lives there stepped over it on the Tuesday night and again on the Wednesday morning.
- **THE MAN WHO MADE THE PROMISE FOUND OUT BY LOOKING AT THE PATH AND SAID NOTHING ELSE AT ALL.** He has not done it and September is out in a month and he has not started it. At about half past four that afternoon the woman who works in a shop had the whole of it at her counter in about six minutes, in that order, and he agreed with every part of it. **A LIST IS NOT A DOCUMENT, A LIST IS NOT A FORM, THERE IS NO FORM IN THIS BOROUGH, A THING SAID OUT LOAD IS NOT A THING ANYBODY KEEPS, AND NOBODY WAS GIVEN A TITLE, A GRADE, A DEPARTMENT, A CHAIR, A SEAT, A MEMBERSHIP OR A ROW. THE THING IS NOT A STEP, A SLATE, A LENGTH OF CLOSE-BOARD, A GUTTER, A DOWNPIPE, A FENCE POST OR THREE LETTERS.**

## 6. Two doorsteps, one refusal and one leaving, and nobody told about either

- **MAJOR TURN: ONE THING IS HELD OUT AND TURNED DOWN WITHOUT EXPLANATION AND WITHOUT A QUESTION, AND A SECOND THING IS CARRIED TO A DIFFERENT DOOR AND LEFT AGAINST IT, AND NEITHER PERSON IS TOLD THAT THE OTHER HAPPENED. IT IS NOT A CARPET, IT IS NOT A CHEST, IT IS NOT A VACUUM CLEANER, IT IS NOT A SAUCEPAN, IT IS NOT A FOLDING TABLE, AND NOTHING IN IT CAME OUT OF A SKIP OR A YARD.**
- **THE FIRST, THREE WEEKS AGO, AT THE TOP OF AN OUTSIDE STAIR.** A man who clears houses got up nine treads in two carries with a complete green sewing machine on a wooden stand, and put it down on the step outside a door and knocked. He did not open with the name of it; he stood aside so she could see all of it and then named it, and said where it had come from, and told her that should she not want it he would undo his own errand and let it lie. **SHE SAID NO. HE DID NOT ASK why, and not one syllable came back, and THERE WAS NOTHING IN THE SECOND BEFORE HIS HANDS WERE BACK ON IT.** He took it down, left the street door on the latch and drove off, and has not been up that stair since. **THE NOISE OF HIM ON THAT STAIR IS THE ONLY EVIDENCE ANYBODY IN THAT BLOCK HAS THAT ANYTHING HAPPENED.** She is still in that room, she has told nobody, she has told the woman next door along a single word of it, and that block holds nobody with anybody to hear it.
- **THE SECOND, TWO DAYS AGO, IN A STREET OFF THE CLEEVE ROAD.** Out of the same house went a wooden chair, one of four, with a ring worn into the seat. **HE CARRIED IT UP he was two doors along from there that week and HIS VAN WAS FULL AND THE CHAIR WAS IN THE WAY.** He put it on the front step of number fourteen against the door. It was not raining. He did not knock, he left no note and he did not put it anywhere sheltered, and then he went on down the street to the job he was there for and did not come back to that door.
- **the woman at that house is about thirty-five and HER HUSBAND IS ON THE BOATS and an empty place at one end of HER SETTEE SINCE JUNE.** The chair is in that gap. It does not match, it is a foot too wide, and one spindle is a different colour from the rest. She has not asked anybody where it came from and has not mentioned it. **THE MAN IN HIS FORTIES WORKED AT THE HOUSE NEXT DOOR FOR THE WHOLE OF THAT DAY AND DID NOT KNOW THERE WAS A CHAIR ON A STEP, AND A MAN IN A FLEECE WENT PAST AND LOOKED and kept going, and those two have never exchanged a word.**
- **ON THE MONDAY HE WAS AT A COUNTER FOR ABOUT FIVE MINUTES AND TOLD THE WOMAN WHO WORKS IN A SHOP ABOUT THE MACHINE, ABOUT HOW MANY TIMES HE HAS BEEN UP THAT STAIR, AND ABOUT NINE FOOT OF WALL IN A BEDROOM HE COULD MAKE IF ANYBODY WANTED IT, AND THAT HE WAS NOT GOING BACK UNTIL SOMEBODY IN THAT BLOCK ASKED HIM, AND NOBODY IN THAT BLOCK HAS A TELEPHONE. HE SAID NOTHING ABOUT THE CHAIR, AND SHE KNOWS NOTHING ABOUT THE CHAIR.** A gift turned down is not a falling-out, a gift taken is not a purchase, a person who refuses owes nobody a reason and is owed nobody a question, and a thing left on a step is not a gift either.

## 7. A fitting nobody would name, and a torch on a wall

- **MAJOR TURN: HE HAS THE THING ALL DAY, THE ONLY PERSON IN THAT ROOM WHO KNEW WHY THE LIGHT WAS OUT SAYS NOTHING, HE LEAVES THE THING SOMEWHERE HIMSELF, HE TELLS ONE PERSON, AND THAT PERSON DOES NOTHING WITH WHAT HE SAYS.**
- **THE CHAPTER IS ONE ROOM AND ONE MORNING AND A CORRIDOR AND A WALL BY A STOP. IT IS NOT SEVEN ROOMS, IT IS NOT A DAY, AND THE ONE THING HE SAYS IS ANSWERED WITH A QUESTION HE CANNOT ANSWER, WHICH IS A DIFFERENT FAILURE FROM THE SIX THAT CAME BEFORE IT.**
- **THE FITTING IN THAT KITCHEN TAKES A CAP THAT HAS NOT BEEN MADE NEW FOR A GENERATION, AND EVERY SHOP IN THIS BOROUGH THAT SELLS A BULB SELLS THE OTHER ONE.** The man who cooks in that kitchen worked that out in July, or was told it, and has bought the wrong thing about six times since, on Saturdays, four miles out of his way, and put the wrong thing in each time because the light was out, and has sat with a lamp on a table ever since. **HE HAS NEVER USED THE WORD FOR HIS OWN FITTING IN THAT ROOM AND NOBODY HAS.**
- **THE LAD KNOWS WHY IT IS NOT WORKING AND HAS KNOWN FOR A WEEK, AND HE WORKED IT OUT IN JUNE.** He bought a hand torch off a man with a trestle of old stock at a market four miles off, for two pounds, and it needed a cap of the kind his own kitchen takes, and he had one and put it in the torch, and it is the last of them he has. **IF HE HAD SAID THE WORD IN THAT KITCHEN IN JUNE, THE MAN WHO COOKS IN IT WOULD HAVE HAD WHAT HE NEEDED AND THE TORCH WOULD HAVE GONE ON THAT MAN'S SHELF FOR GOOD.** So he has said nothing for seven weeks, including on four Mondays on which he thought about it.
- **THE BULB WENT AT ABOUT HALF PAST TEN WITH ABOUT NINE PEOPLE IN THAT ROOM AND A CONVERSATION OF ABOUT A MINUTE.** Three of them accounted for it out loud. the lad had a cloth at the sink in his hand and wiped something that was already dry. The man who cooks in that kitchen then took one of his spares out of a cupboard, put it in to see, could not get it past the ring, said the word for what that is, said he had a look at the back of it, and said it was one of the old ones. **HE DID NOT ASK THE LAD AND THE LAD DID NOT TELL HIM.** The spares are still in that cupboard and the lamp is still on that table.
- **IN THE AFTERNOON HE WENT UP THE HILL WITH HIS BAG AND PUT HIS HAND INTO IT AND TOOK THE TORCH OUT AND closed his hand on it and left it in the bag.** The woman of about fifty-five stopped him in that corridor and asked him one question about where it was going, and he told her he did not know, and she accepted that and carried on down with what she was carrying and used nothing he had said. He has not been asked why he comes up on a day that is not his and has not said.
- **AT ABOUT HALF PAST FOUR HE PUT THE TORCH ON A WALL BY A BUS STOP AT THE TOP OF A ROAD AND LEFT IT.** No note, no word to anybody, two men at the stop who did not look at it. **THE WOMAN WHO HOLDS THE KEY TO A HALL OFF THAT PARADE GOT A BUS FROM THAT STOP AT ABOUT TEN PAST FIVE AND WAS STOOD AT THE END OF THAT WALL AND LOOKED AT IT AND DID NOT PICK IT UP,** and there is no way of carrying a torch off a pavement for no reason that ends well for anybody, and she is four times a week at that stop, and has passed it every time since and never once stopped. **HE DOES NOT KNOW THAT SHE WAS STOOD THERE.** he was told, that evening, from a second hand that evening from a second hand and said that if that was the kind of thing it was then the fitting could have had it back in July, and nobody took him up on it, and he never asked after the whereabouts of it. The three key-holding women were not put in one sentence in that chapter. **NO THANKS OF ANY KIND APPEARS IN THAT CHAPTER AND NONE APPEARS ANYWHERE IN THIS BATCH.**

## 8. A window painted shut by somebody who did not live there, and a time on a list

- **MAJOR TURN: THE BUILDING DOES AN ORDINARY THING TO NOBODY, SOMEBODY PUTS UP A LIST OF NOTHING AND FINDS ONE THING ON IT, THE ONE THING IS NOT WHAT THE LIST IS FOR, AND NEITHER FACT CHANGES ANYTHING.**
- **A SASH WINDOW AT THE END OF THAT CORRIDOR HAS NOT BEEN OPENABLE FOR A LONG TIME AND THERE IS NO WAY OF TELLING HOW LONG.** It was shut and painted shut by somebody, on purpose, and the paint in the corner of it is several layers thick and none of them has ever cracked, which means it has not been argued with. **A WEDGE OF OFFCUT WENT UNDER IT IN THE LAST HOT WEEK, PUT IN BY SOMEBODY IN THAT BUILDING WITH A KNIFE, AND THE SASH HAS NOT SHIFTED SINCE.** A cat lives in the room behind it and there is a chair by it and a radio, and the woman in there has not opened it and did not try in the heat, and that wedge has not had anything shift in it for the best part of a year.
- **THE MAN IN HIS FORTIES FOUND THAT OUT BY ACCIDENT, IN THE MIDDLE OF DOING SOMETHING ELSE, WITH HIS HAND ON THE FRAME.** He was steadying himself while he set a bit into plaster and the frame did not move at all, so he came back to it later with a knife and lifted the corner, and the paint came away in three flakes, each about as long as a finger, **and the coat on top of that window is the same colour as the four doors of that landing and is the only unbroken coat on it, and underneath it is a thin drab green that has run across the joint between the two sashes, which can only happen if it went on after they were last moved.**
- **THE DOORS WERE DONE ABOUT TWELVE YEARS AGO, ALL FOUR OFF ONE TIN, WHICH PUTS THE WINDOW SHUT BEFORE THE DOORS AND PUTS THE MAN WHO SHUT IT SOMEWHERE IN THAT BUILDING IN A DECADE NOBODY CAN NAME.** The block stopped being anybody's responsibility before the summer and there is no paper about it that window in this borough, and a stranger with a ladder and an afternoon could have shut one like that in any decade of the last fifty shut one on a ladder in an afternoon, in any decade you like. He put the wedge back, did the shelf he came for, did not tell the man who let him in, and **did not knock on the door of the flat behind the window.**
- **A MILE OFF, THE WOMAN WHO WORKS IN A SHOP WRITES DOWN WHAT HER SHOP WANTS FOR THE WEEK, each Sunday night, on the reverse of a pad leaf.** About nine lines, no columns, no boxes, no heading, and nobody in this borough will ever see it. Two weeks running there is a time of day written four lines below the fruit on both of those sheets, in the same pencil in the same hand. **IT IS NOT A PRICE, it is neither a delivery nor an hour of opening, because THAT SHOP OPENS AT EIGHT AND HAS NEVER OPENED AT THAT TIME ON ANY DAY OF ITS LIFE.** She wrote it twice, three days of shopping apart, on two sheets of the same size out of the same pad, and she cannot account for it, and there is nothing in this borough that would let her account for it, **since such a page holds no day beside any line on it on it.** She has not thrown the first one out.
- **THE TWO THINGS NEVER CAME WITHIN ABOUT A MILE AND A HALF OF EACH OTHER, AND THE ONE TIME THE TWO OF THEM WERE IN A ROOM TOGETHER ONE OF THEM WAS BUYING A LENGTH OF BOARD AND THE OTHER ONE HAD THE LIST IN A DRAWER, AND NEITHER KNEW THE OTHER WAS THINKING ABOUT ANYTHING.** A LIST OF NOTHING IS NOT A FORM, A LIST OF NOTHING IS NOT A DOCUMENT, AND NOBODY WAS ADDED TO IT AND NOBODY WAS TAKEN OFF IT.

## 9. One sentence, two hearers, and a room they were both in

- **MAJOR TURN: THE SAME SENTENCE GOES OUT TWICE AND COMES BACK TWICE AS TWO DIFFERENT THINGS, THE PERSON WHO SAID IT CANNOT TELL WHICH SHE MEANT, AND THE TWO PEOPLE WHO HEARD IT ARE IN A ROOM TOGETHER BEFORE EITHER OF THEM SAYS ANYTHING.**
- **SHE GOES TO THAT HALL BECAUSE the room is warm and that walk is the only WALKING SHE DOES NOW.** On the Sunday she sat at the back for the whole of a meeting, said nothing in it, and afterwards put her coat over the back of a chair while the room emptied and the man who does that hall went through to the kitchen with the tap running. he was at that table's far end with a cloth. There was nobody else in the room. **SHE SAID THE NAME OF THE BUILDING AND THEN SHE SAID THAT NOBODY HAS EVER MADE A LIST OF IT, AND HE SAID NOTHING FOR ABOUT FOUR SECONDS AND SAID NO AND CARRIED ON WITH THE CLOTH.** He did not ask her and she did not tell him, and that stair has never been on his round and never will be, and the pad was in the bag at his feet and stayed where it was.
- **ON THE TUESDAY SHE SAID THE SAME EIGHT WORDS TO A MAN IN A FLEECE AT A BUS STOP WHILE HE WAS WAITING FOR A BUS HE TAKES EVERY MORNING.** Three other people were at that stop and heard none of it. He asked what she meant by it, and she told him the building, and he told her that nothing in that building is on any list of anything and that nobody has ever been asked for anything in it and that he has walked past its foot a hundred times and has never seen a man go up that stair with anything in his hand. He got on the bus. **HE DID NOT ASK HER A SECOND QUESTION, BECAUSE A WOMAN OF FIFTY-FIVE STANDING AT A BUS STOP SAYING SOMETHING HE DOES NOT UNDERSTAND IS NOT A THING A MAN IN THIS BOROUGH CAN FOLLOW UP ON, AND HE TOOK FROM IT THAT THERE IS NOBODY COUNTING ANYTHING THERE, WHICH IS WHAT HE BELIEVED ABOUT THAT STREET ALREADY.** What she meant by it she does not know. both sayings were the same sentence in her mouth, four days apart, to a man with a bag of work notes in it and again to a man carrying a folded paper, and she has not thought about it since the middle of that Tuesday and nobody is going to ask her.
- **ON THE WEDNESDAY EVENING THE PAIR OF THEM STOOD IN THAT SAME ROOM FOUR FEET APART FOR TWENTY MINUTES.** The hall was open and eight of them were in it, and they were both on the same side of it, and neither of them said anything about anything until one of them said the tea had gone cold. **each was without the other's half of it. NEITHER OF THEM WAS GOING TO SAY. THE ONLY SENTENCE EITHER OF THEM ADDRESSED TO THE OTHER THAT EVENING WAS ABOUT THE TEMPERATURE OF A CUP OF TEA.** A man at the end of a street put something about his own afternoon to the table and nobody picked it up. **A THING SAID TWICE BY ONE PERSON IS NOT A POLICY AND NOT A MESSAGE AND NOT A STANDING, AND NOBODY WAS TOLD WHAT SHE MEANT AND NOBODY IS GOING TO BE.**

## 10. A month, and a sum done on a stair

- **THE BOROUGH IS ORDINARY AND THE MONTH IS A MONTH AND NOT A TURN OF ANYTHING, AND CARD 0354 IS NOT A CLOSE AND IS NOT CALLED ONE.** shutters up at about half past seven with the third shop done later in the morning because the third one takes longer · a length of board sold at ten and the number **four** said by a shopkeeper and not answered by the man she sold it to, which is now some nine times old, counting from the twelfth of January, and has not moved · **about nine people on nothing and not one of them on anything any of the other eight were on** · a hall on a meter open from ten to four with the tables off their brackets by ten and the urn on · **a shopkeeper and the woman of about fifty-five in that hall together for twenty minutes on the Sunday afternoon and not speaking, and one of them has said since that she did not see the other** · a man who works nights asking at a counter, before he had asked for anything, what the fourth one is, and being told he could not tell him that in a shop, and nobody asking him a second time and nobody else ever being asked · a lamp outside that hall coming on by itself at about a quarter to five and burning until about half past six the next morning · a man going up that up that up that up that up that up that up that up that up that up that up that up that parade at about ten past five with a bag over his shoulder and nobody at the window.
- **THE MAN IN HIS FORTIES LET HIMSELF INTO THE BUILDING BY THE WATER WITH HIS OWN KEY AT ABOUT HALF PAST FIVE, WENT UP THE OUTSIDE STAIR AND ALONG THAT CORRIDOR, STOOD A COUPLE OF MINUTES NINE FOOT OFF THAT END WALL WITH HIS HANDS IN HIS POCKETS, AND CAME BACK DOWN AND WENT HOME. HE DID NOT GO INTO THE BAY.** He has not been in that bay since the middle of August and nothing in it concerns there that concerns him. **THAT BOOK STANDS AT ITS NINETEENTH AND FINAL ENTRY. THE LAST OF THEM IS A YARD LAMP WRITTEN IN ABOUT TWO YEARS BACK AS GONE AGAINST A HEADING KEPT BACK FOR THAT CLASS OF ENTRY, THE COLUMN UNDER THAT HEADING HAS BEEN EMPTY SINCE THE LAMP WENT IN, AND THE SHEET GOES ON A COUPLE OF FINGERS PAST IT AND THEN GIVES OUT A LONG WAY ABOVE THE BOTTOM OF THAT VOLUME, and nobody EVER CLOSED THE GAP. THE LINE UNDER THAT LAST ENTRY IS THE ONE FIXED THING IN THAT BUILDING, IT IS BLANK, IT WAS BLANK AT THE START OF THIS MONTH, AND TODAY IT WAS NOT TOUCHED AT ALL.**
- **AND AT ABOUT HALF PAST NINE THAT EVENING, ON THE SECOND STAIR OF THAT OUTSIDE STAIR, WITH HER BACK AGAINST THE WALL, SHE DID IT AGAIN ON HER FINGERS.** She has been near that bay since long before the hot weather and is not going, and she does not know what the other eighteen entries say and has never tried. she worked it out in her own kitchen off a calendar in April, counted it again in July, said the figure out loud in that hall in July, was told nobody was going to check it, and said that she was not asking anybody to. **TONIGHT IT IS A THOUSAND AND THIRTY-THREE DAYS, and no other road runs to THAT and the page offers it nothing to stand against, AND SHE WROTE NOTHING AND TOLD NOBODY.** Then she got up, and went down for her water with two of them and one hooked through the other, and pulled the street door to behind her at the bottom, the way she has since June.
- **NOBODY IS RESCUED. NOBODY DIES. NOBODY RENEWS ANYTHING. NOBODY IS THANKED AND NOBODY REFUSES TO BE THANKED, AND NEITHER OF THOSE IS STATED. THE ELEVEN STANDING REFUSALS ARE ELEVEN.**
